					<h1 id="firstHeading" class="firstHeading mw-first-heading"><span class="mw-page-title-main">Martin Van Buren</span></h1>
							
<div id="p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown mw-portlet mw-portlet-lang"  >
	<input type="checkbox" id="p-lang-btn-checkbox" role="button" aria-haspopup="true" data-event-name="ui.dropdown-p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown-checkbox mw-interlanguage-selector" aria-label="Go to an article in another language. Available in 129 languages"   >
	<label id="p-lang-btn-label" for="p-lang-btn-checkbox" class="vector-dropdown-label cdx-button cdx-button--fake-button cdx-button--fake-button--enabled cdx-button--weight-quiet cdx-button--action-progressive mw-portlet-lang-heading-129" aria-hidden="true"  ><span class="vector-icon mw-ui-icon-language-progressive mw-ui-icon-wikimedia-language-progressive"></span>

<span class="vector-dropdown-label-text">129 languages</span>
	</label>
	<div class="vector-dropdown-content">

		<div class="vector-menu-content">
			
			<ul class="vector-menu-content-list">
				
				<li class="interlanguage-link interwiki-af mw-list-item"><a href="https://af.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Van_Buren" title="Martin Van Buren – Afrikaans" lang="af" hreflang="af" 					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">President of the United States from 1837 to 1841</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Van Buren" redirects here. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Van_Buren_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Van Buren (disambiguation)">Van Buren (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">In this <a href="/wiki/Dutch_name" title="Dutch name">Dutch name</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Surname" title="Surname">surname</a> is <i> Van Buren</i>, not <i> Buren</i>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">

<p><b>Martin Van Buren</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="&#39;v&#39; in &#39;vie&#39;">v</span><span title="/æ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;bad&#39;">æ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span></span><span class="wrap"> </span><span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;b&#39; in &#39;buy&#39;">b</span><span title="/jʊər/: &#39;ure&#39; in &#39;cure&#39;">jʊər</span><span title="/ən/: &#39;on&#39; in &#39;button&#39;">ən</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling">van <span style="font-size:90%">BURE</span>-ən</i></a>; <a href="/wiki/Dutch_language" title="Dutch language">Dutch</a>: <i lang="nl">Maarten van Buren</i> <small></small><span class="IPA nowrap" lang="nl-Latn-fonipa" title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Dutch" title="Help:IPA/Dutch">[ˈmaːrtə(n)<span class="wrap"> </span>vɑm<span class="wrap"> </span>ˈbyːrə(n)]</a></span>&#32;<span data-nosnippet="" id="ooui-php-1" class="ext-phonos ext-phonos-PhonosButton noexcerpt ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel oo-ui-widget oo-ui-widget-enabled oo-ui-buttonElement oo-ui-buttonElement-frameless oo-ui-iconElement oo-ui-buttonWidget" data-ooui="{&quot;_&quot;:&quot;mw.Phonos.PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;\/\/upload.wikimedia.org\/wikipedia\/commons\/transcoded\/7\/76\/Maarten_van_Buren.ogg\/Maarten_van_Buren.ogg.mp3&quot;,&quot;rel&quot;:[&quot;nofollow&quot;],&quot;framed&quot;:false,&quot;icon&quot;:&quot;volumeUp&quot;,&quot;data&quot;:{&quot;ipa&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;text&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;en&quot;,&quot;wikibase&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;file&quot;:&quot;Maarten van Buren.ogg&quot;},&quot;classes&quot;:[&quot;ext-phonos&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;noexcerpt&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel&quot;]}"><a role="button" tabindex="0" href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/7/76/Maarten_van_Buren.ogg/Maarten_van_Buren.ogg.mp3" rel="nofollow" aria-label="Play audio" class="oo-ui-buttonElement-button"><span class="oo-ui-iconElement-icon oo-ui-icon-volumeUp"></span><span class="oo-ui-labelElement-label"></span><span class="oo-ui-indicatorElement-indicator oo-ui-indicatorElement-noIndicator"></span></a></span><sup class="ext-phonos-attribution noexcerpt"><a href="/wiki/File:Maarten_van_Buren.ogg" title="File:Maarten van Buren.ogg">i</a></sup>; December 5, 1782 – July 24, 1862) was an American lawyer, diplomat, and statesman who served as the eighth <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">president of the United States</a> from 1837 to 1841. A primary founder of the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States)" title="Democratic Party (United States)">Democratic Party</a>, he served as <a href="/wiki/Attorney_General_of_New_York" title="Attorney General of New York">New York's attorney general</a>, <a href="/wiki/U.S._Senator" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. Senator">U.S. senator</a>, then briefly as the ninth <a href="/wiki/Governor_of_New_York" title="Governor of New York">governor of New York</a> before joining <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a>'s administration as the tenth <a href="/wiki/United_States_secretary_of_state" class="mw-redirect" title="United States secretary of state">United States secretary of state</a>, minister to Great Britain, and ultimately the eighth <a href="/wiki/Vice_President_of_the_United_States" title="Vice President of the United States">vice president</a> when named Jackson's running mate for the <a href="/wiki/1832_United_States_presidential_election" title="1832 United States presidential election">1832 election</a>. Van Buren won the presidency in <a href="/wiki/1836_United_States_presidential_election" title="1836 United States presidential election">1836</a>, lost re-election in 1840, and failed to win the Democratic nomination in 1844. Later in his life, Van Buren emerged as an elder statesman and an important anti-slavery leader who led the <a href="/wiki/Free_Soil_Party" title="Free Soil Party">Free Soil Party</a> ticket in the <a href="/wiki/1848_United_States_presidential_election" title="1848 United States presidential election">1848 presidential election</a>.
</p><p>Van Buren was born in <a href="/wiki/Kinderhook_(village),_New_York" title="Kinderhook (village), New York">Kinderhook, New York</a>, where most residents were of <a href="/wiki/Dutch_people" title="Dutch people">Dutch</a> descent and spoke Dutch as their primary language. He was the first president to have been born after the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolution" title="American Revolution">American Revolution</a>,  in which his father served as a <a href="/wiki/Patriot_(American_Revolution)" title="Patriot (American Revolution)">patriot</a>. He is the only president to have spoken English as a second language. Trained as a lawyer, he entered politics as a member of the <a href="/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party" title="Democratic-Republican Party">Democratic-Republican Party</a>, won a seat in the <a href="/wiki/New_York_State_Senate" title="New York State Senate">New York State Senate</a>, and was elected to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate" title="United States Senate">United States Senate</a> in 1821. As the leader of the <a href="/wiki/Bucktails" title="Bucktails">Bucktails</a> faction, Van Buren emerged as the most influential politician from New York in the 1820s and established a political machine known as the <a href="/wiki/Albany_Regency" title="Albany Regency">Albany Regency</a>.
</p><p>Following the <a href="/wiki/1824_United_States_presidential_election" title="1824 United States presidential election">1824 presidential election</a>, Van Buren sought to re-establish a two-party system with partisan differences based on ideology rather than personalities or sectional differences; he supported Andrew Jackson's candidacy in the <a href="/wiki/1828_United_States_presidential_election" title="1828 United States presidential election">1828 presidential election</a> with this goal in mind. He ran successfully for governor of New York to support Jackson's campaign but resigned shortly after Jackson was inaugurated so he could accept appointment as Jackson's secretary of state. In the cabinet, Van Buren was a key Jackson advisor and built the organizational structure for the coalescing Democratic Party. He ultimately resigned to help resolve the <a href="/wiki/Petticoat_affair" title="Petticoat affair">Petticoat affair</a> and briefly served as ambassador to Great Britain. At Jackson's behest, the <a href="/wiki/1832_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1832 Democratic National Convention">1832 Democratic National Convention</a> nominated Van Buren for vice president, and he took office after the Democratic ticket won the 1832 presidential election.
</p><p>With Jackson's strong support and the organizational strength of the Democratic Party, Van Buren successfully ran for president in the 1836 presidential election, defeating several Whig opponents. However, his popularity soon eroded because of his response to the <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1837" title="Panic of 1837">Panic of 1837</a>, which centered on his <a href="/wiki/Independent_Treasury" title="Independent Treasury">Independent Treasury</a> system, a plan under which the federal government of the United States would store its funds in vaults rather than in banks; more conservative Democrats and Whigs in Congress ultimately delayed his plan from being implemented until 1840. His presidency was further marred by the costly <a href="/wiki/Second_Seminole_War" title="Second Seminole War">Second Seminole War</a> (a result of continuing Jackson's <a href="/wiki/Indian_removal" title="Indian removal">Indian removal</a> policy); and his refusal to admit Texas to the Union as a <a href="/wiki/Slave_states_and_free_states" title="Slave states and free states">slave state</a> as an attempt to avoid heightened sectional tensions. In 1840, Van Buren lost his <a href="/wiki/1840_United_States_presidential_election" title="1840 United States presidential election">re-election bid</a> to <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison" title="William Henry Harrison">William Henry Harrison</a>, the nominee of the anti-<a href="/wiki/Jacksonian_democracy" title="Jacksonian democracy">Jacksonian</a> <a href="/wiki/Whig_Party_(United_States)" title="Whig Party (United States)">Whig Party</a>.
</p><p>Van Buren was initially the leading candidate for the Democratic Party's nomination again in 1844, but his continued opposition to the <a href="/wiki/Texas_annexation" title="Texas annexation">annexation of Texas</a> angered Southern Democrats, leading to the nomination of <a href="/wiki/James_K._Polk" title="James K. Polk">James K. Polk</a>. Growing opposed to slavery, Van Buren was the newly formed Free Soil Party's presidential nominee in 1848, and his candidacy helped Whig nominee <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor" title="Zachary Taylor">Zachary Taylor</a> defeat Democrat <a href="/wiki/Lewis_Cass" title="Lewis Cass">Lewis Cass</a>. Worried about sectional tensions, Van Buren returned to the Democratic Party after 1848, but was disappointed with the pro-southern presidencies of Franklin Pierce and James Buchanan. He supported the policies of President <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln" title="Abraham Lincoln">Abraham Lincoln</a>, a Republican, during the <a href="/wiki/American_Civil_War" title="American Civil War">American Civil War</a>. He died of asthma at his home in Kinderhook in 1862, aged 79.
</p><p>While Van Buren is praised for anti-slavery stances, in historical rankings, historians and political scientists often <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">rank Van Buren as an average or below-average U.S. president</a>, due to his handling of the Panic of 1837.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_education">Early life and education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life and education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Family_of_Martin_Van_Buren" title="Family of Martin Van Buren">Family of Martin Van Buren</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:%22The_Birthplace_of_President_Martin_Van_Buren_in_Kinderhook,_New_York%22,_by_J.W._Barber.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/%22The_Birthplace_of_President_Martin_Van_Buren_in_Kinderhook%2C_New_York%22%2C_by_J.W._Barber.jpg/220px-%22The_Birthplace_of_President_Martin_Van_Buren_in_Kinderhook%2C_New_York%22%2C_by_J.W._Barber.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/%22The_Birthplace_of_President_Martin_Van_Buren_in_Kinderhook%2C_New_York%22%2C_by_J.W._Barber.jpg/330px-%22The_Birthplace_of_President_Martin_Van_Buren_in_Kinderhook%2C_New_York%22%2C_by_J.W._Barber.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/5/5f/%22The_Birthplace_of_President_Martin_Van_Buren_in_Kinderhook%2C_New_York%22%2C_by_J.W._Barber.jpg 2x" data-file-width="420" data-file-height="280" /></a><figcaption>Van Buren's birthplace by <a href="/wiki/John_Warner_Barber" title="John Warner Barber">John Warner Barber</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Martin_Van_Buren_Baptism_record.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Martin_Van_Buren_Baptism_record.jpg/170px-Martin_Van_Buren_Baptism_record.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Martin_Van_Buren_Baptism_record.jpg/255px-Martin_Van_Buren_Baptism_record.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Martin_Van_Buren_Baptism_record.jpg/340px-Martin_Van_Buren_Baptism_record.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3240" data-file-height="4320" /></a><figcaption>Baptism record indicating the Dutch spelling of Van Buren's first name, "Maarten"</figcaption></figure>
<p>Martin Van Buren was born <sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> on Thursday, December 5, 1782, in <a href="/wiki/Kinderhook_(town),_New_York" class="mw-redirect" title="Kinderhook (town), New York">Kinderhook</a>, New York, about 20 miles (32&#160;km) south of <a href="/wiki/Albany,_New_York" title="Albany, New York">Albany</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Hudson_River" title="Hudson River">Hudson River</a> valley.
</p><p>His father, <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Van_Buren_(I)" class="mw-redirect" title="Abraham Van Buren (I)">Abraham Van Buren</a>, was a descendant of Cornelis Maessen, a native of <a href="/wiki/Buurmalsen" title="Buurmalsen">Buurmalsen, Netherlands</a> who had emigrated to <a href="/wiki/New_Netherland" title="New Netherland">New Netherland</a> in 1631 and purchased a plot of land on <a href="/wiki/Manhattan_Island" class="mw-redirect" title="Manhattan Island">Manhattan Island</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECole19843,_9_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECole19843,_9-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWidmer2005153–165_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWidmer2005153–165-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren was the first U.S President without any British ancestry.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Abraham Van Buren had been a <a href="/wiki/Patriot_(American_Revolution)" title="Patriot (American Revolution)">Patriot</a> during the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolution" title="American Revolution">American Revolution</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> and he later joined the <a href="/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party" title="Democratic-Republican Party">Democratic-Republican Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> He owned an inn and tavern in Kinderhook and served as Kinderhook's town clerk for several years. In 1776, he married Maria Hoes (or Goes) Van Alen (1746-1818) in the <a href="/wiki/Kinderhook_(town),_New_York" class="mw-redirect" title="Kinderhook (town), New York">town of Kinderhook</a>, also of Dutch extraction and the widow of Johannes Van Alen (1744-c. 1773). She had three children from her first marriage, including future U.S. Representative <a href="/wiki/James_I._Van_Alen" title="James I. Van Alen">James I. Van Alen</a>. Her second marriage produced five children, of which Martin was the third.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven19835–6_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven19835–6-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Van Buren received a basic education at the village schoolhouse, and briefly studied <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Kinderhook_Academy" class="mw-redirect" title="Kinderhook Academy">Kinderhook Academy</a> and at <a href="/wiki/Claverack_College" title="Claverack College">Washington Seminary</a> in <a href="/wiki/Claverack,_New_York" title="Claverack, New York">Claverack</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECole198414_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECole198414-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren was raised speaking primarily Dutch and learned English while attending school;<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> he is the only president of the United States whose first language was not English.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWidmer20056–7_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWidmer20056–7-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Also during his childhood, Van Buren learned at his father's inn how to interact with people from varied ethnic, income, and societal groups, which he used to his advantage as a political organizer.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> His formal education ended in 1796, when he began <a href="/wiki/Reading_law" title="Reading law">reading law</a> at the office of <a href="/wiki/Peter_Silvester_(1734%E2%80%931808)" title="Peter Silvester (1734–1808)">Peter Silvester</a> and his son Francis.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke2010230_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke2010230-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Van Buren, at 5&#160;feet 6&#160;inches (1.68&#160;m) tall, was small in stature, and affectionately nicknamed "Little Van".<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> When he began his legal studies he wore rough, homespun clothing,<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> causing the Silvesters to admonish him to pay greater heed to his clothing and personal appearance as an aspiring lawyer. He accepted their advice, and subsequently emulated the Silvesters' clothing, appearance, bearing, and conduct.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> The lessons he learned from the Silvesters were reflected in his career as a lawyer and politician, in which Van Buren was known for his amiability and fastidious appearance.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Despite Kinderhook's strong affiliation with the <a href="/wiki/Federalist_Party" title="Federalist Party">Federalist Party</a>, of which the Silvesters were also strong supporters, Van Buren adopted his father's Democratic-Republican leanings.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven19838–9_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven19838–9-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> The Silvesters and Democratic-Republican political figure <a href="/wiki/John_Peter_Van_Ness" title="John Peter Van Ness">John Peter Van Ness</a> suggested that Van Buren's political leanings constrained him to complete his education with a Democratic-Republican attorney, so he spent a final year of apprenticeship in the New York City office of John Van Ness's brother <a href="/wiki/William_P._Van_Ness" title="William P. Van Ness">William P. Van Ness</a>, a political lieutenant of <a href="/wiki/Aaron_Burr" title="Aaron Burr">Aaron Burr</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198310–11_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198310–11-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Van Ness introduced Van Buren to the intricacies of New York state politics, and Van Buren observed Burr's battles for control of the state Democratic-Republican party against <a href="/wiki/George_Clinton_(vice_president)" title="George Clinton (vice president)">George Clinton</a> and <a href="/wiki/Robert_R._Livingston_(chancellor)" class="mw-redirect" title="Robert R. Livingston (chancellor)">Robert R. Livingston</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198314–15_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198314–15-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> He returned to Kinderhook in 1803, after his <a href="/wiki/Admission_to_the_bar_in_the_United_States" title="Admission to the bar in the United States">admission to the New York bar</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198315–17_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198315–17-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Hannah_van_buren_2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Hannah_van_buren_2.jpg/180px-Hannah_van_buren_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="249" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Hannah_van_buren_2.jpg/270px-Hannah_van_buren_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Hannah_van_buren_2.jpg/360px-Hannah_van_buren_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1155" data-file-height="1600" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Hannah_Van_Buren" title="Hannah Van Buren">Hannah Van Buren</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Van Buren married <a href="/wiki/Hannah_Van_Buren" title="Hannah Van Buren">Hannah Hoes</a> (or Goes) in <a href="/wiki/Catskill,_New_York" class="mw-redirect" title="Catskill, New York">Catskill, New York</a>, on February 21, 1807. She was his childhood sweetheart and a daughter of his maternal first cousin, Johannes Dircksen Hoes.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> She grew up in <a href="/wiki/Valatie,_New_York" title="Valatie, New York">Valatie</a>, and like Van Buren her home life was primarily Dutch; she spoke Dutch as her first language, and spoke English with a marked accent.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> The couple had six children, four of whom lived to adulthood: <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Van_Buren" title="Abraham Van Buren">Abraham</a> (1807–1873), unnamed daughter (stillborn around 1809), <a href="/wiki/John_Van_Buren" title="John Van Buren">John</a> (1810–1866), Martin Jr. (1812–1855), Winfield Scott (born and died in 1814), and Smith Thompson (1817–1876).<sup id="cite_ref-MVLiB_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MVLiB-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Hannah contracted <a href="/wiki/Tuberculosis" title="Tuberculosis">tuberculosis</a>, and died in Kinderhook on February 5, 1819, at age 35.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESilbey200227_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESilbey200227-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren never remarried.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career">Early political career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early political career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Martin_Van_Buren_1.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Martin_Van_Buren_1.png/180px-Martin_Van_Buren_1.png" decoding="async" width="180" height="240" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Martin_Van_Buren_1.png/270px-Martin_Van_Buren_1.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Martin_Van_Buren_1.png/360px-Martin_Van_Buren_1.png 2x" data-file-width="1068" data-file-height="1424" /></a><figcaption>Painting of Van Buren by Daniel Dickinson, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1820s</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>Upon returning to Kinderhook in 1803, Van Buren formed a law partnership with his half-brother, James Van Alen, and became financially secure enough to increase his focus on politics.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren had been active in politics from age&#160;18, if not before. In 1801, he attended a <a href="/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party" title="Democratic-Republican Party">Democratic-Republican Party</a> convention in <a href="/wiki/Troy,_New_York" title="Troy, New York">Troy, New York</a>, where he worked successfully to secure for John Peter Van Ness the party nomination in a special election for the <a href="/wiki/New_York%27s_6th_congressional_district" title="New York&#39;s 6th congressional district">6th Congressional District</a> seat.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrooke2010283_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrooke2010283-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Upon returning to Kinderhook, Van Buren broke with the Burr faction, becoming an ally of both <a href="/wiki/DeWitt_Clinton" title="DeWitt Clinton">DeWitt Clinton</a> and <a href="/wiki/Daniel_D._Tompkins" title="Daniel D. Tompkins">Daniel D. Tompkins</a>. After the faction led by Clinton and Tompkins dominated the 1807 elections, Van Buren was appointed <a href="/wiki/Probate_court" title="Probate court">Surrogate</a> of <a href="/wiki/Columbia_County,_New_York" title="Columbia County, New York">Columbia County, New York</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198318–22_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198318–22-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Seeking a better base for his political and legal career, Van Buren and his family moved to the town of <a href="/wiki/Hudson,_New_York" title="Hudson, New York">Hudson</a>, the seat of Columbia County, in 1808.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198323–24_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198323–24-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren's legal practice continued to flourish, and he traveled all over the state to represent various clients.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198325–26_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198325–26-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1812, Van Buren won his party's nomination for a seat in the <a href="/wiki/New_York_State_Senate" title="New York State Senate">New York State Senate</a>. Though several Democratic-Republicans, including John Peter Van Ness, joined with the Federalists to oppose his candidacy, Van Buren won election to the state senate in mid-1812.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198329–32_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198329–32-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Later in the year, the United States entered the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a> against Great Britain, while Clinton launched an unsuccessful bid to defeat President <a href="/wiki/James_Madison" title="James Madison">James Madison</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1812_United_States_presidential_election" title="1812 United States presidential election">1812 presidential election</a>. After the election, Van Buren became suspicious that Clinton was working with the Federalist Party, and he broke from his former political ally.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198333–39_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198333–39-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the War of 1812, Van Buren worked with Clinton, Governor Tompkins, and <a href="/wiki/Ambrose_Spencer" title="Ambrose Spencer">Ambrose Spencer</a> to support the Madison administration's prosecution of the war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198341–42_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198341–42-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In addition, he was a special judge advocate appointed to serve as a prosecutor of <a href="/wiki/William_Hull" title="William Hull">William Hull</a> during Hull's court-martial following the <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Detroit" title="Siege of Detroit">surrender</a> of Detroit.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Anticipating another military campaign, he collaborated with <a href="/wiki/Winfield_Scott" title="Winfield Scott">Winfield Scott</a> on ways to reorganize the New York Militia in the winter of 1814–1815, but the end of the war halted their work in early 1815.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren was so favorably impressed by Scott that he named his fourth son after him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198343_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198343-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren's strong support for the war boosted his standing, and in 1815, he was elected to the position of <a href="/wiki/New_York_Attorney_General" class="mw-redirect" title="New York Attorney General">New York Attorney General</a>. Van Buren moved from Hudson to the state capital of <a href="/wiki/Albany,_New_York" title="Albany, New York">Albany</a>, where he established a legal partnership with <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin_Butler_(lawyer)" title="Benjamin Franklin Butler (lawyer)">Benjamin Butler</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198348–50_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198348–50-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> and shared a house with political ally <a href="/wiki/Roger_Skinner" title="Roger Skinner">Roger Skinner</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> In 1816, Van Buren won re-election to the state senate, and he would continue to simultaneously serve as both state senator and as the state's attorney general.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198351–52_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198351–52-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> In 1819, he played an active part in prosecuting the accused murderers of Richard Jennings, the first murder-for-hire case in the state of New York.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Albany_regency">Albany regency</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Albany regency">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After Tompkins was elected as vice president in the <a href="/wiki/1816_United_States_presidential_election" title="1816 United States presidential election">1816 presidential election</a>, Clinton defeated Van Buren's preferred candidate, <a href="/wiki/Peter_Buell_Porter" title="Peter Buell Porter">Peter Buell Porter</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/New_York_gubernatorial_election,_1817" class="mw-redirect" title="New York gubernatorial election, 1817">1817 New York gubernatorial election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198358–59_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198358–59-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Clinton threw his influence behind the construction of the <a href="/wiki/Erie_Canal" title="Erie Canal">Erie Canal</a>, an ambitious project designed to connect <a href="/wiki/Lake_Erie" title="Lake Erie">Lake Erie</a> to the Atlantic Ocean.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198361–62_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198361–62-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Though many of Van Buren's allies urged him to block Clinton's Erie Canal bill, Van Buren believed that the canal would benefit the state. His support for the bill helped it win approval from the New York legislature.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198363–64_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198363–64-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Despite his support for the Erie Canal, Van Buren became the leader of an anti-Clintonian faction in New York known as the "<a href="/wiki/Bucktails" title="Bucktails">Bucktails</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198362_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198362-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Bucktails succeeded in emphasizing party loyalty and used it to capture and control many patronage posts throughout New York. Through his use of patronage, loyal newspapers, and connections with local party officials and leaders, Van Buren established what became known as the "<a href="/wiki/Albany_Regency" title="Albany Regency">Albany Regency</a>", a <a href="/wiki/Political_machine" title="Political machine">political machine</a> that emerged as an important factor in New York politics.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198364–65_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198364–65-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> The Regency relied on a coalition of small farmers, but also enjoyed support from the <a href="/wiki/Tammany_Hall" title="Tammany Hall">Tammany Hall</a> machine in New York City.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983110_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983110-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> During this era, Van Buren largely determined Tammany Hall's political policy for New York's Democratic-Republicans.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELazo30_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELazo30-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A New York state referendum that expanded state voting rights to all white men in 1821, and which further increased the power of Tammany Hall, was guided by Van Buren.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Although Governor Clinton remained in office until late 1822, Van Buren emerged as the leader of the state's Democratic-Republicans after the 1820 elections.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198381–82_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198381–82-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren was a member of the 1820 state <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_convention_(political_meeting)" class="mw-redirect" title="Constitutional convention (political meeting)">constitutional convention</a>, where he favored expanded voting rights, but opposed universal suffrage and tried to maintain property requirements for voting.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Entry_into_national_politics">Entry into national politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Entry into national politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In February 1821, the state legislature <a href="/wiki/1821_United_States_Senate_election_in_New_York" title="1821 United States Senate election in New York">elected</a> Van Buren to represent New York in the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate" title="United States Senate">United States Senate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven198392–93_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven198392–93-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren arrived in Washington during the "<a href="/wiki/Era_of_Good_Feelings" title="Era of Good Feelings">Era of Good Feelings</a>", a period in which partisan distinctions at the national level had faded.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson198426–27_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson198426–27-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren quickly became a prominent figure in Washington, D.C., befriending Secretary of the Treasury <a href="/wiki/William_H._Crawford" title="William H. Crawford">William H. Crawford</a>, among others.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983117_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983117-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Though not an exceptional orator, Van Buren frequently spoke on the Senate floor, usually after extensively researching the subject at hand. Despite his commitments as a father and state party leader, Van Buren remained closely engaged in his legislative duties, and during his time in the Senate he served as the chairman of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_Finance" title="United States Senate Committee on Finance">Senate Finance Committee</a> and the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_the_Judiciary" title="United States Senate Committee on the Judiciary">Senate Judiciary Committee</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983176_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983176-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> As he gained renown, Van Buren earned monikers like "Little Magician" and "Sly Fox".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECole1984432_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECole1984432-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Van Buren chose to back Crawford over <a href="/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams" title="John Quincy Adams">John Quincy Adams</a>, <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Henry_Clay" title="Henry Clay">Henry Clay</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1824_United_States_presidential_election" title="1824 United States presidential election">presidential election of 1824</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983126–127_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983126–127-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Crawford shared Van Buren's affinity for <a href="/wiki/Jeffersonian_democracy" title="Jeffersonian democracy">Jeffersonian</a> principles of <a href="/wiki/States%27_rights" title="States&#39; rights">states' rights</a> and limited government, and Van Buren believed that Crawford was the ideal figure to lead a coalition of New York, Pennsylvania, and Virginia's "<a href="/wiki/Richmond_Junto" class="mw-redirect" title="Richmond Junto">Richmond Junto</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983129–130_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983129–130-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren's support for Crawford aroused strong opposition in New York in the form of the People's party, which drew support from Clintonians, Federalists, and others opposed to Van Buren.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983139–140_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983139–140-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Nonetheless, Van Buren helped Crawford win the Democratic-Republican party's presidential nomination at the February&#160;1824 <a href="/wiki/Congressional_nominating_caucus" title="Congressional nominating caucus">congressional nominating caucus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983143–144_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983143–144-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> The other Democratic-Republican candidates in the race refused to accept the poorly attended caucus's decision, and as the Federalist Party had all but ceased to function as a national party, the 1824 campaign became a competition among four candidates of the same party. Though Crawford suffered a severe stroke that left him in poor health, Van Buren continued to support his chosen candidate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983145–146_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983145–146-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren met with <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson" title="Thomas Jefferson">Thomas Jefferson</a> in May&#160;1824 in an attempt to bolster Crawford's candidacy, and though he was unsuccessful in gaining a public endorsement for Crawford, he nonetheless cherished the chance to meet with his political hero.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983146–148_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983146–148-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The 1824 elections dealt a severe blow to the Albany Regency, as Clinton returned to the governorship with the support of the People's party. By the time the state legislature convened to choose the state's <a href="/wiki/Electoral_College_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Electoral College (United States)">presidential electors</a>, results from other states had made it clear that no individual would win a majority of the electoral vote, necessitating a <a href="/wiki/Contingent_election" title="Contingent election">contingent election</a> in the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="United States House of Representatives">United States House of Representatives</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983151–152_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983151–152-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> While Adams and Jackson finished in the top three and were eligible for selection in the contingent election, New York's electors would help determine whether Clay or Crawford would finish third.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983152–153_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983152–153-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Though most of the state's electoral votes went to Adams, Crawford won one more electoral vote than Clay in the state, and Clay's defeat in Louisiana left Crawford in third place.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983156–157_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983156–157-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> With Crawford still in the running, Van Buren lobbied members of the House to support him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983157–158_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983157–158-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> He hoped to engineer a Crawford victory on the second ballot of the contingent election, but Adams won on the first ballot with the help of Clay and <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Van_Rensselaer" title="Stephen Van Rensselaer">Stephen Van Rensselaer</a>, a Congressman from New York. Despite his close ties with Van Buren, Van Rensselaer cast his vote for Adams, thus giving Adams a narrow majority of New York's delegation and a victory in the contingent election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983158,_160–161_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983158,_160–161-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the House contest, Van Buren shrewdly kept out of the controversy which followed, and began looking forward to 1828. Jackson was angered to see the presidency go to Adams despite having won more popular votes than he had, and he eagerly looked forward to a rematch.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESilbey200244_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESilbey200244-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Jackson's supporters accused Adams and Clay of having made a "<a href="/wiki/Corrupt_bargain" title="Corrupt bargain">corrupt bargain</a>" in which Clay helped Adams win the contingent election in return for Clay's appointment as <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State" title="United States Secretary of State">Secretary of State</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983164–165_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983164–165-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren was always courteous in his treatment of opponents and showed no bitterness toward either Adams or Clay, and he voted to confirm Clay's nomination to the cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> At the same time, Van Buren opposed the Adams-Clay plans for <a href="/wiki/Internal_improvements" title="Internal improvements">internal improvements</a> like roads and canals and declined to support U.S. participation in the <a href="/wiki/Congress_of_Panama" title="Congress of Panama">Congress of Panama</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECole1984111_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECole1984111-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren considered Adams's proposals to represent a return to the <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton" title="Alexander Hamilton">Hamiltonian</a> economic model favored by Federalists, which he strongly opposed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983163–164_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983163–164-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> Despite his opposition to Adams's public policies, Van Buren easily secured re-election in his divided home state in 1827.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983177–178_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983177–178-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1828_elections">1828 elections</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: 1828 elections">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Van Buren's overarching goal at the national level was to restore a two-party system with party cleavages based on philosophical differences, and he viewed the old divide between Federalists and Democratic-Republicans as beneficial to the nation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson198427–28_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson198427–28-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren believed that these national parties helped ensure that elections were decided on national, rather than sectional or local, issues; as he put it, "party attachment in former times furnished a complete antidote for sectional prejudices". After the 1824 election, Van Buren was initially somewhat skeptical of Jackson, who had not taken strong positions on most policy issues. Nonetheless, he settled on Jackson as the one candidate who could beat Adams in the <a href="/wiki/1828_United_States_presidential_election" title="1828 United States presidential election">1828 presidential election</a>, and he worked to bring Crawford's former backers into line behind Jackson.
</p><p>He also forged alliances with other members of Congress opposed to Adams, including Vice President <a href="/wiki/John_C._Calhoun" title="John C. Calhoun">John C. Calhoun</a>, Senator <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Hart_Benton_(politician)" title="Thomas Hart Benton (politician)">Thomas Hart Benton</a>, and Senator <a href="/wiki/John_Randolph_of_Roanoke" title="John Randolph of Roanoke">John Randolph</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983178–181_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983178–181-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Seeking to solidify his standing in New York and bolster Jackson's campaign, Van Buren helped arrange the passage of the <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_Abominations" title="Tariff of Abominations">Tariff of 1828</a>, which opponents labeled as the "Tariff of Abominations". The tariff was intended to protect northern and western agricultural products from foreign competition, but the resulting tax on imports cut into the profits of New England businessmen, who imported raw materials including iron to make into finished goods for subsequent resale, as well as finished goods including clothing for distribution and resale throughout the United States. The tariff also raised the cost of living in the South, because Southern states had little manufacturing capacity, which required them to export raw materials including cotton or sell them to Northern manufacturers, then import higher-priced finished goods or purchase them from the North. The tariff thus satisfied many who sought <a href="/wiki/Protectionism" title="Protectionism">protection</a> from foreign competition, but angered Southern cotton interests and New England importers and manufacturers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983196–199_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983196–199-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Because Van Buren believed the South would never support Adams, and New England would never support Jackson, he was willing to alienate both regions through passage of the tariff.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983201_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983201-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Meanwhile, Clinton's death from a heart attack in 1828 dramatically shook up the politics of Van Buren's home state, while the <a href="/wiki/Anti-Masonic_Party" title="Anti-Masonic Party">Anti-Masonic Party</a> emerged as an increasingly important factor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983194–196_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983194–196-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> After some initial reluctance, Van Buren chose to run for Governor of New York in the <a href="/wiki/New_York_gubernatorial_election,_1828" class="mw-redirect" title="New York gubernatorial election, 1828">1828 election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983202–203,_209_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983202–203,_209-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Hoping that a Jackson victory would lead to his elevation to Secretary of State or Secretary of the Treasury, Van Buren chose <a href="/wiki/Enos_T._Throop" title="Enos T. Throop">Enos T. Throop</a> as his running mate and preferred successor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983209_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983209-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren's candidacy was aided by the split between supporters of Adams, who had adopted the label of <a href="/wiki/National_Republicans" class="mw-redirect" title="National Republicans">National Republicans</a>, and the Anti-Masonic Party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983206–208_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983206–208-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Reflecting his public association with Jackson, Van Buren accepted the gubernatorial nomination on a ticket that called itself "Jacksonian-Democrat".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983211_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983211-90">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> He campaigned on local as well as national issues, emphasizing his opposition to the policies of the Adams administration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983211–212_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983211–212-91">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren ran ahead of Jackson, winning the state by 30,000&#160;votes compared to a margin of 5,000 for Jackson.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983214–215_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983214–215-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Nationally, Jackson defeated Adams by a wide margin, winning nearly every state outside of New England.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson19849_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson19849-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> After the election, Van Buren resigned from the Senate to start his term as governor, which began on January&#160;1, 1829.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> While his term as governor was short, he did manage to pass the Bank Safety Fund Law, an early form of deposit insurance, through the legislature.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> He also appointed several key supporters, including <a href="/wiki/William_L._Marcy" title="William L. Marcy">William L. Marcy</a> and <a href="/wiki/Silas_Wright" title="Silas Wright">Silas Wright</a>, to important state positions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983218–219_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983218–219-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Jackson_administration_.281829.E2.80.931837.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Jackson_administration_(1829–1837)">Jackson administration (1829–1837)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Jackson administration (1829–1837)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Secretary_of_State">Secretary of State</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Secretary of State">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Petticoat_affair" title="Petticoat affair">Petticoat affair</a> and <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_Andrew_Jackson" title="Presidency of Andrew Jackson">Presidency of Andrew Jackson</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Floride_Calhoun_nee_Colhoun.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/62/Floride_Calhoun_nee_Colhoun.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="296" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="200" data-file-height="296" /></a><figcaption>Mrs <a href="/wiki/Floride_Calhoun" title="Floride Calhoun">Floride Calhoun</a>, a leader of the "petticoats"</figcaption></figure>
<p>In February 1829, Jackson wrote to Van Buren to ask him to become Secretary of State.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983228_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983228-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren quickly agreed, and he resigned as governor the following month; his tenure of forty-three days is the shortest of any Governor of New York.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983229–231_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983229–231-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> No serious diplomatic crises arose during Van Buren's tenure as Secretary of State, but he achieved several notable successes, such as settling long-standing claims against France and winning reparations for property that had been seized during the <a href="/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars" title="Napoleonic Wars">Napoleonic Wars</a>. He reached an agreement with the British to open trade with the <a href="/wiki/British_West_Indies" title="British West Indies">British West Indies</a> colonies and concluded a treaty with the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a> that gained American merchants access to the <a href="/wiki/Black_Sea" title="Black Sea">Black Sea</a>. Items on which he did not achieve success included settling the <a href="/wiki/Maine" title="Maine">Maine</a>-<a href="/wiki/New_Brunswick" title="New Brunswick">New Brunswick</a> boundary dispute with Great Britain, gaining settlement of the U.S. claim to the <a href="/wiki/Oregon_Country" title="Oregon Country">Oregon Country</a>, concluding a commercial treaty with Russia, and persuading Mexico to sell <a href="/wiki/Texas" title="Texas">Texas</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECole1984203_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECole1984203-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-MVB_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MVB-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In addition to his foreign policy duties, Van Buren quickly emerged as an important advisor to Jackson on major domestic issues like the tariff and internal improvements.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983250–252_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983250–252-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> The Secretary of State was instrumental in convincing Jackson to issue the <a href="/wiki/Maysville_Road_veto" title="Maysville Road veto">Maysville Road veto</a>, which both reaffirmed limited government principles and also helped prevent the construction of infrastructure projects that could potentially compete with New York's Erie Canal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983261–262_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983261–262-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> He also became involved in a power struggle with Calhoun over appointments and other issues, including the <a href="/wiki/Petticoat_Affair" class="mw-redirect" title="Petticoat Affair">Petticoat Affair</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983245–247_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983245–247-103">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> The Petticoat Affair arose because <a href="/wiki/Peggy_Eaton" title="Peggy Eaton">Peggy Eaton</a>, wife of <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_War" title="United States Secretary of War">Secretary of War</a> <a href="/wiki/John_H._Eaton" class="mw-redirect" title="John H. Eaton">John H. Eaton</a>, was ostracized by the other cabinet wives due to the circumstances of her marriage.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Led by <a href="/wiki/Floride_Calhoun" title="Floride Calhoun">Floride Calhoun</a>, wife of Vice President John Calhoun, the other cabinet wives refused to pay courtesy calls to the Eatons, receive them as visitors, or invite them to social events.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEManweller2012&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksiduqB3ehA7M0oCpgPA232_232&#93;_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEManweller2012[httpsbooksgooglecombooksiduqB3ehA7M0oCpgPA232_232]-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> As a widower, Van Buren was unaffected by the position of the cabinet wives.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren initially sought to mend the divide in the cabinet, but most of the leading citizens in Washington continued to snub the Eatons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983249–253_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983249–253-108">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> Jackson was close to Eaton, and he came to the conclusion that the allegations against Eaton arose from a plot against his administration led by Henry Clay.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983249,_253_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983249,_253-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> The Petticoat Affair, combined with a contentious debate over the tariff and Calhoun's decade-old criticisms of Jackson's actions in the <a href="/wiki/First_Seminole_War" class="mw-redirect" title="First Seminole War">First Seminole War</a>, contributed to a split between Jackson and Calhoun.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson19849–10_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson19849–10-110">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> As the debate over the tariff and the proposed ability of South Carolina to <a href="/wiki/Nullification_(U.S._Constitution)" title="Nullification (U.S. Constitution)">nullify</a> federal law consumed Washington, Van Buren, not Calhoun, increasingly emerged as Jackson's likely successor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983266_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983266-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Petticoat affair was finally resolved when Van Buren offered to resign. In April 1831, Jackson accepted and reorganized his cabinet by asking for the resignations of the anti-Eaton cabinet members.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Postmaster General <a href="/wiki/William_T._Barry" title="William T. Barry">William T. Barry</a>, who had sided with the Eatons in the Petticoat Affair, was the lone cabinet member to remain in office.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> The cabinet reorganization removed Calhoun's allies from the Jackson administration, and Van Buren had a major role in shaping the new cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983266,_269–270_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983266,_269–270-114">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> After leaving office, Van Buren continued to play a part in the <a href="/wiki/Kitchen_Cabinet" title="Kitchen Cabinet">Kitchen Cabinet</a>, Jackson's informal circle of advisors.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEManweller2012&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksiduqB3ehA7M0oCpgPA246_246&#93;_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEManweller2012[httpsbooksgooglecombooksiduqB3ehA7M0oCpgPA246_246]-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ambassador_to_Britain_and_vice_presidency">Ambassador to Britain and vice presidency</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Ambassador to Britain and vice presidency">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Francis_Alexander_-_Martin_Van_Buren_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Francis_Alexander_-_Martin_Van_Buren_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/180px-Francis_Alexander_-_Martin_Van_Buren_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Francis_Alexander_-_Martin_Van_Buren_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/270px-Francis_Alexander_-_Martin_Van_Buren_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Francis_Alexander_-_Martin_Van_Buren_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/360px-Francis_Alexander_-_Martin_Van_Buren_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3733" data-file-height="4706" /></a><figcaption>A painting of Van Buren by <a href="/wiki/Francis_Alexander" title="Francis Alexander">Francis Alexander</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr>&#160;1830</figcaption></figure>
<p>In August 1831, Jackson gave Van Buren a <a href="/wiki/Recess_appointment" title="Recess appointment">recess appointment</a> as the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Ambassador to the United Kingdom">ambassador to Britain</a>, and Van Buren arrived in London in September.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEShepard1899224,_248_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShepard1899224,_248-116">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> He was cordially received, but in February&#160;1832, he learned that the Senate had rejected his nomination.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> The rejection of Van Buren was essentially the work of Calhoun.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECole1984124_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECole1984124-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> When the vote on Van Buren's nomination was taken, enough pro-Calhoun Jacksonians refrained from voting to produce a tie, which allowed Calhoun to cast the deciding vote against Van Buren.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Calhoun was elated, convinced that he had ended Van Buren's career. "It will kill him dead, sir, kill him dead. He will never kick, sir, never kick", Calhoun exclaimed to a friend.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007378_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007378-120">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> Calhoun's move backfired; by making Van Buren appear the victim of petty politics, Calhoun raised Van Buren in both Jackson's regard and the esteem of others in the Democratic Party. Far from ending Van Buren's career, Calhoun's action gave greater impetus to Van Buren's candidacy for vice president.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Seeking to ensure that Van Buren would replace Calhoun as his running mate, Jackson had arranged for a national convention of his supporters.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson198410–11_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson198410–11-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> The May&#160;<a href="/wiki/1832_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1832 Democratic National Convention">1832 Democratic National Convention</a> subsequently nominated Van Buren to serve as the party's vice presidential nominee.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESilbey200281–82_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESilbey200281–82-123">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren won the nomination over <a href="/wiki/Philip_P._Barbour" title="Philip P. Barbour">Philip P. Barbour</a> (Calhoun's favored candidate) and <a href="/wiki/Richard_Mentor_Johnson" title="Richard Mentor Johnson">Richard Mentor Johnson</a> due to the support of Jackson and the strength of the Albany Regency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983300–301_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983300–301-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> Upon Van Buren's return from Europe in July&#160;1832, he became involved in the <a href="/wiki/Bank_War" title="Bank War">Bank War</a>, a struggle over the renewal of the charter of the <a href="/wiki/Second_Bank_of_the_United_States" title="Second Bank of the United States">Second Bank of the United States</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983301–303_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983301–303-125">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Van Buren had long been distrustful of banks, and he viewed the bank as an extension of the Hamiltonian economic program, so he supported Jackson's veto of the bank's re-charter.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983303,_306–307_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983303,_306–307-126">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Henry Clay, the presidential nominee of the National Republicans, made the struggle over the bank the key issue of the <a href="/wiki/U.S._presidential_election,_1832" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. presidential election, 1832">presidential election of 1832</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983307–308_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983307–308-127">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> The Jackson–Van Buren ticket won the 1832 election by a landslide,<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> and Van Buren took office as vice president in March&#160;1833.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> During the <a href="/wiki/Nullification_Crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Nullification Crisis">Nullification Crisis</a>, Van Buren counseled Jackson to pursue a policy of conciliation with South Carolina leaders.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983321–322_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983321–322-130">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> He played little direct role in the passage of the <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_1833" title="Tariff of 1833">Tariff of 1833</a>, but he quietly hoped that the tariff would help bring an end to the Nullification Crisis, which it did.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983328_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983328-131">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As vice president, Van Buren continued to be one of Jackson's primary advisors and confidants, and accompanied Jackson on his tour of the northeastern United States in 1833.<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> Jackson's struggle with the Second Bank of the United States continued, as the president sought to remove federal funds from the bank.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983330–332_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983330–332-133">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Though initially apprehensive of the removal due to congressional support for the bank, Van Buren eventually came to support Jackson's policy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983330–334_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983330–334-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> He also helped undermine a fledgling alliance between Jackson and <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Webster" title="Daniel Webster">Daniel Webster</a>, a senator from Massachusetts who could have potentially threatened Van Buren's project to create two parties separated by policy differences rather than personalities.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983355–358_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983355–358-135">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> During Jackson's second term, the president's supporters began to refer to themselves as members of the Democratic Party. Meanwhile, those opposed to Jackson, including Clay's National Republicans, followers of Calhoun and Webster, and many members of the Anti-Masonic Party, coalesced into the <a href="/wiki/Whig_Party_(United_States)" title="Whig Party (United States)">Whig Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson198414–15_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson198414–15-136">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidential_election_of_1836">Presidential election of 1836</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Presidential election of 1836">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/1836_United_States_presidential_election" title="1836 United States presidential election">1836 United States presidential election</a> and <a href="/wiki/Second_Party_System" title="Second Party System">Second Party System</a></div>
<p>President <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a> declined to seek another term in the <a href="/wiki/1836_United_States_presidential_election" title="1836 United States presidential election">1836 presidential election</a>, but he remained influential within the <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_United_States_Democratic_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="History of the United States Democratic Party">Democratic Party</a> as his second term came to an end. Jackson was determined to help elect Van Buren in 1836 so that the latter could continue the Jackson administration's policies. The two men—the charismatic "Old Hickory" and the efficient "Sly Fox"—had entirely different personalities but had become an effective team in eight years in office together.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> With Jackson's support, Van Buren won the presidential nomination of the <a href="/wiki/1835_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1835 Democratic National Convention">1835 Democratic National Convention</a> without opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-JRIrelan_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JRIrelan-138">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> Two names were put forward for the vice-presidential nomination: Representative <a href="/wiki/Richard_M._Johnson" class="mw-redirect" title="Richard M. Johnson">Richard M. Johnson</a> of Kentucky, and former Senator <a href="/wiki/William_Cabell_Rives" title="William Cabell Rives">William Cabell Rives</a> of Virginia. Southern Democrats, and Van Buren himself, strongly preferred Rives. Jackson, on the other hand, strongly preferred Johnson. Again, Jackson's considerable influence prevailed, and Johnson received the required two-thirds vote after New York Senator <a href="/wiki/Silas_Wright" title="Silas Wright">Silas Wright</a> prevailed upon non-delegate Edward Rucker to cast the 15 votes of the absent Tennessee delegation in Johnson's favor.<sup id="cite_ref-RMJ9th_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RMJ9th-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-JRIrelan_138-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JRIrelan-138">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ElectoralCollege1836.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/ElectoralCollege1836.svg/250px-ElectoralCollege1836.svg.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="257" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/ElectoralCollege1836.svg/375px-ElectoralCollege1836.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/ElectoralCollege1836.svg/500px-ElectoralCollege1836.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="577" data-file-height="593" /></a><figcaption>1836 electoral vote results</figcaption></figure>
<p>Van Buren's competitors in the <a href="/wiki/1836_United_States_presidential_election" title="1836 United States presidential election">election of 1836</a> were three members of the Whig Party, which remained a loose coalition bound by mutual opposition to Jackson's anti-bank policies. Lacking the party unity or organizational strength to field a single ticket or define a single platform,<sup id="cite_ref-RMJ9th_139-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RMJ9th-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> the Whigs ran several regional candidates in hopes of sending the election to the House of Representatives.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> The three candidates were <a href="/wiki/Hugh_Lawson_White" title="Hugh Lawson White">Hugh Lawson White</a> of Tennessee, Daniel Webster of Massachusetts, and <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison" title="William Henry Harrison">William Henry Harrison</a> of Indiana. Besides endorsing <a href="/wiki/Internal_improvements" title="Internal improvements">internal improvements</a> and a national bank, the Whigs tried to tie Democrats to <a href="/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States" title="Abolitionism in the United States">abolitionism</a> and sectional tension, and attacked Jackson for "acts of aggression and usurpation of power".<sup id="cite_ref-preselections_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-preselections-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Southern voters represented the biggest potential impediment to Van Buren's quest for the presidency, as many were apprehensive at the prospect of a Northern president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson198417–18_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson198417–18-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren moved to obtain their support by assuring them that he opposed abolitionism and supported maintaining slavery in states where it already existed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007508–509_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007508–509-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> To demonstrate consistency regarding his opinions on slavery, Van Buren cast the tie-breaking Senate vote for a bill to subject abolitionist mail to state laws, thus ensuring that its circulation would be prohibited in the South.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren considered slavery to be immoral but sanctioned by the Constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Van Buren won the election with 764,198 popular votes, 50.9% of the total, and 170&#160;<a href="/wiki/Electoral_College_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Electoral College (United States)">electoral votes</a>. Harrison led the Whigs with 73&#160;electoral votes, White receiving 26, and Webster&#160;14.<sup id="cite_ref-preselections_141-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-preselections-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Willie_Person_Mangum" class="mw-redirect" title="Willie Person Mangum">Willie Person Mangum</a> received South Carolina's 11 electoral votes, which were awarded by the state legislature.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007487_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007487-146">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren's victory resulted from a combination of his attractive political and personal qualities, Jackson's popularity and endorsement, the organizational power of the Democratic Party, and the inability of the Whig Party to muster an effective candidate and campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-MVelections_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MVelections-147">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Virginia's presidential electors voted for Van Buren for president, but voted for <a href="/wiki/William_Smith_(South_Carolina_senator)" title="William Smith (South Carolina senator)">William Smith</a> for vice president, leaving Johnson one electoral vote short of election.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> In accordance with the <a href="/wiki/Twelfth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution" title="Twelfth Amendment to the United States Constitution">Twelfth Amendment</a>, the Senate elected Johnson vice president in a contingent vote.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The election of 1836 marked an important turning point in American political history because it saw the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/Second_Party_System" title="Second Party System">Second Party System</a>. In the early 1830s, the political party structure was still changing, rapidly, and factional and personal leaders continued to play a major role in politics. By the end of the campaign of 1836, the new <a href="/wiki/Second_Party_System" title="Second Party System">Second Party System</a> was almost complete, as nearly every faction had been absorbed by either the Democrats or the Whigs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECole1984279_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECole1984279-150">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Presidency_.281837.E2.80.931841.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidency_(1837–1841)">Presidency (1837–1841)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Presidency (1837–1841)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_Martin_Van_Buren" title="Presidency of Martin Van Buren">Presidency of Martin Van Buren</a></div>
