<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cabinet">Cabinet</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Cabinet">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_Martin_Van_Buren#Cabinet" title="Presidency of Martin Van Buren">Presidency of Martin Van Buren §&#160;Cabinet</a></div>
<p>Van Buren retained much of Jackson's cabinet and lower-level appointees, as he hoped that the retention of Jackson's appointees would stop Whig momentum in the South and restore confidence in the Democrats as a party of sectional unity.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENowlan2012320_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENowlan2012320-151">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> The cabinet holdovers represented the different regions of the country: Secretary of the Treasury <a href="/wiki/Levi_Woodbury" title="Levi Woodbury">Levi Woodbury</a> came from New England, Attorney General <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin_Butler_(lawyer)" title="Benjamin Franklin Butler (lawyer)">Benjamin Franklin Butler</a> and Secretary of the Navy <a href="/wiki/Mahlon_Dickerson" title="Mahlon Dickerson">Mahlon Dickerson</a> hailed from New York and New Jersey, respectively, Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/John_Forsyth_(Georgia)" class="mw-redirect" title="John Forsyth (Georgia)">John Forsyth</a> of Georgia represented the South, and Postmaster General <a href="/wiki/Amos_Kendall" title="Amos Kendall">Amos Kendall</a> of Kentucky represented the West.
</p><p>For the lone open position of Secretary of War, Van Buren first approached William Cabell Rives, who had sought the vice presidency in 1836. After Rives declined to join the cabinet, Van Buren appointed <a href="/wiki/Joel_Roberts_Poinsett" title="Joel Roberts Poinsett">Joel Roberts Poinsett</a>, a South Carolinian who had opposed secession during the <a href="/wiki/Nullification_Crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Nullification Crisis">Nullification Crisis</a>. Van Buren's cabinet choices were criticized by Pennsylvanians such as <a href="/wiki/James_Buchanan" title="James Buchanan">James Buchanan</a>, who argued that their state deserved a cabinet position as well as some Democrats who argued that Van Buren should have used his patronage powers to augment his power. However, Van Buren saw value in avoiding contentious patronage battles, and his decision to retain Jackson's cabinet made it clear that he intended to continue the policies of his predecessor. Additionally, Van Buren had helped select Jackson's cabinet appointees and enjoyed strong working relationships with them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson198437–40_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson198437–40-152">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Van Buren held regular formal cabinet meetings and discontinued the informal gatherings of advisors that had attracted so much attention during Jackson's presidency. He solicited advice from department heads, tolerated open and even frank exchanges between cabinet members, perceiving himself as "a mediator, and to some extent an umpire between the conflicting opinions" of his counselors. Such detachment allowed the president to reserve judgment and protect his prerogative for making final decisions. These open discussions gave cabinet members a sense of participation and made them feel part of a functioning entity, rather than isolated executive agents.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENowlan2012321_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENowlan2012321-153">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren was closely involved in foreign affairs and matters pertaining to the Treasury Department; but the Post Office, War Department, and Navy Department had significant autonomy under their respective cabinet secretaries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson1984171_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1984171-154">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Panic_of_1837">Panic of 1837</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Panic of 1837">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1837" title="Panic of 1837">Panic of 1837</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Martin_Van_Buren_MET_ap93.19.2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Martin_Van_Buren_MET_ap93.19.2.jpg/180px-Martin_Van_Buren_MET_ap93.19.2.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="217" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Martin_Van_Buren_MET_ap93.19.2.jpg/270px-Martin_Van_Buren_MET_ap93.19.2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Martin_Van_Buren_MET_ap93.19.2.jpg/360px-Martin_Van_Buren_MET_ap93.19.2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1920" data-file-height="2313" /></a><figcaption>Painting of Van Buren by <a href="/wiki/Henry_Inman_(painter)" title="Henry Inman (painter)">Henry Inman</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1837–1838</span></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jackson_and_Van_Buren,_1837.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Jackson_and_Van_Buren%2C_1837.jpg/220px-Jackson_and_Van_Buren%2C_1837.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="152" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Jackson_and_Van_Buren%2C_1837.jpg/330px-Jackson_and_Van_Buren%2C_1837.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Jackson_and_Van_Buren%2C_1837.jpg/440px-Jackson_and_Van_Buren%2C_1837.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1854" data-file-height="1277" /></a><figcaption><i>The modern balaam and his ass</i>, an 1837 caricature placing the blame for the Panic of 1837 and the perilous state of the banking system on outgoing President Andrew Jackson, shown riding a donkey, while President Martin Van Buren comments approvingly</figcaption></figure>
<p>When Van Buren entered office, the nation's economic health had taken a turn for the worse and the prosperity of the early 1830s was over. Two months into his presidency, on May&#160;10, 1837, some important state banks in New York, running out of hard currency reserves, refused to convert paper money into gold or silver, and other financial institutions throughout the nation quickly followed suit. This <a href="/wiki/Financial_crisis" title="Financial crisis">financial crisis</a> would become known as the <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1837" title="Panic of 1837">Panic of 1837</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-MBdomestic_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MBdomestic-155">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> The Panic was followed by a five-year <a href="/wiki/Depression_(economics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Depression (economics)">depression</a> in which banks failed and unemployment reached record highs.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Van Buren blamed the economic collapse on greedy American and foreign business and financial institutions, as well as the over-extension of credit by U.S. banks. Whig leaders in Congress blamed the Democrats, along with Andrew Jackson's economic policies,<sup id="cite_ref-MBdomestic_155-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MBdomestic-155">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> specifically his 1836 <a href="/wiki/Specie_Circular" title="Specie Circular">Specie Circular</a>. Cries of "rescind the circular!" went up and former president Jackson sent word to Van Buren asking him not to rescind the order, believing that it had to be given enough time to work. Others, like <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_Biddle_(banker)" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicholas Biddle (banker)">Nicholas Biddle</a>, believed that Jackson's <a href="/wiki/Bank_War#Jackson&#39;s_dismantling_of_the_BUS" title="Bank War">dismantling of the Bank of the United States</a> was directly responsible for the irresponsible creation of paper money by the state banks which had precipitated this panic.<sup id="cite_ref-Seigenthaler_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Seigenthaler-157">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> The Panic of 1837 loomed large over the <a href="/wiki/United_States_elections,_1838" class="mw-redirect" title="United States elections, 1838">1838 election cycle</a>, as the carryover effects of the economic downturn led to Whig gains in both the U.S. House and Senate. The state elections in 1837 and 1838 were also disastrous for the Democrats,<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> and the partial economic recovery in 1838 was offset by a second commercial crisis later that year.<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>To address the crisis, the Whigs proposed rechartering the <a href="/wiki/History_of_central_banking_in_the_United_States" title="History of central banking in the United States">national bank</a>. The president countered by proposing the establishment of an <a href="/wiki/Independent_Treasury" title="Independent Treasury">independent U.S. treasury</a>, which he contended would take the politics out of the nation's money supply. Under the plan, the government would hold its money in <a href="/wiki/Hard_money_(policy)" title="Hard money (policy)">gold or silver</a>, and would be restricted from printing <a href="/wiki/Banknote" title="Banknote">paper money</a> at will; both measures were designed to prevent inflation.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> The plan would permanently separate the government from private banks by storing government funds in government vaults rather than in private banks.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson198458–62_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson198458–62-161">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren announced his proposal in September&#160;1837,<sup id="cite_ref-MBdomestic_155-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MBdomestic-155">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> but an alliance of conservative Democrats and Whigs prevented it from becoming law until 1840.<sup id="cite_ref-Morrison456_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Morrison456-162">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> As the debate continued, conservative Democrats like Rives defected to the Whig Party, which itself grew more unified in its opposition to Van Buren.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson198461–62_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson198461–62-163">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> The Whigs would abolish the Independent Treasury system in 1841, but it was revived in 1846, and remained in place until the passage of the <a href="/wiki/Federal_Reserve_Act" title="Federal Reserve Act">Federal Reserve Act</a> in 1913.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson1984210_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1984210-164">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> More important for Van Buren's immediate future, the depression would be a major issue in his upcoming re-election campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-MBdomestic_155-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MBdomestic-155">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Indian_removal">Indian removal</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Indian removal">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Federal policy under Jackson had sought to move Indian tribes to lands west of the <a href="/wiki/Mississippi_River" title="Mississippi River">Mississippi River</a> through the <a href="/wiki/Indian_Removal_Act_of_1830" class="mw-redirect" title="Indian Removal Act of 1830">Indian Removal Act of 1830</a>, and the federal government negotiated 19&#160;treaties with Indian tribes during Van Buren's presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-MVforcebehind_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MVforcebehind-165">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> The 1835 <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_New_Echota" title="Treaty of New Echota">Treaty of New Echota</a> signed by government officials and representatives of the <a href="/wiki/Cherokee" title="Cherokee">Cherokee</a> tribe had established terms under which the Cherokees ceded their territory in the southeast and agreed to move west to <a href="/wiki/Oklahoma" title="Oklahoma">Oklahoma</a>. In 1838, Van Buren directed General <a href="/wiki/Winfield_Scott" title="Winfield Scott">Winfield Scott</a> to forcibly move all those who had not yet complied with the treaty.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Cherokees were herded violently into <a href="/wiki/Internment_camp" class="mw-redirect" title="Internment camp">internment camps</a> where they were kept for the summer of 1838. The actual transportation west was delayed by intense heat and drought, but in the fall, the Cherokee reluctantly agreed to transport themselves west.<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> Some 20,000&#160;people were relocated against their will during the Cherokee removal, part of the <a href="/wiki/Trail_of_Tears" title="Trail of Tears">Trail of Tears</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-newnetherland_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-newnetherland-169">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> Notably, <a href="/wiki/Ralph_Waldo_Emerson" title="Ralph Waldo Emerson">Ralph Waldo Emerson</a>, who would go on to become America's foremost man of letters, wrote Van Buren <a href="/wiki/Emerson%27s_letter_to_Martin_Van_Buren" title="Emerson&#39;s letter to Martin Van Buren">a letter</a> protesting his treatment of the Cherokee.<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Seminole_War_in_Everglades.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Seminole_War_in_Everglades.jpg/220px-Seminole_War_in_Everglades.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Seminole_War_in_Everglades.jpg/330px-Seminole_War_in_Everglades.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Seminole_War_in_Everglades.jpg/440px-Seminole_War_in_Everglades.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2864" data-file-height="1915" /></a><figcaption>A United States Marine Corps boat expedition searching the <a href="/wiki/Everglades" title="Everglades">Everglades</a> during the Second Seminole War</figcaption></figure>
<p>President Jackson used the army to force military troops to force <a href="/wiki/Seminole" title="Seminole">Seminole</a> Indians in Florida to move to the west. Many  did surrender but they then escaped from detention camps. In December 1837, in the <a href="/wiki/Second_Seminole_War" title="Second Seminole War">Second Seminole War</a> the army launched a massive offensive, leading to the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Lake_Okeechobee" title="Battle of Lake Okeechobee">Battle of Lake Okeechobee</a> and a new phase of attrition.  Realizing it was almost impossible to remove the remaining Seminoles from Florida, the administration negotiated a compromise  allowing them to remain in southwest Florida.<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Texas">Texas</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Texas">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Just before leaving office in March 1837, Andrew Jackson extended diplomatic recognition to the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Texas" title="Republic of Texas">Republic of Texas</a>, which had won independence from Mexico in the <a href="/wiki/Texas_Revolution" title="Texas Revolution">Texas Revolution</a>. By suggesting the prospect of quick <a href="/wiki/Annexation" title="Annexation">annexation</a>, Jackson raised the danger of war with Mexico and heightened sectional tensions at home. New England abolitionists charged that there was a "<a href="/wiki/Slave_Power" title="Slave Power">slaveholding conspiracy</a> to acquire Texas", and <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Webster" title="Daniel Webster">Daniel Webster</a> eloquently denounced annexation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHenretta2004&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksid3djEYV3R7oICpgPA109_109&#93;_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHenretta2004[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid3djEYV3R7oICpgPA109_109]-172">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> Many Southern leaders, meanwhile, strongly desired the expansion of slave-holding territory in the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson1984151–152_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1984151–152-173">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Boldly reversing Jackson's policies, Van Buren sought peace abroad and harmony at home. He proposed a diplomatic solution to a long-standing financial dispute between American citizens and the Mexican government, rejecting Jackson's threat to settle it by force.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHenretta2004&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksid3djEYV3R7oICpgPA109_109&#93;_172-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHenretta2004[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid3djEYV3R7oICpgPA109_109]-172">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> Likewise, when the Texas minister at Washington, D.C., proposed annexation to the administration in August&#160;1837, he was told that the proposition could not be entertained. Constitutional scruples and fear of war with Mexico were the reasons given for the rejection,<sup id="cite_ref-TexAnnex3638_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TexAnnex3638-174">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> but concern that it would precipitate a clash over the extension of slavery undoubtedly influenced Van Buren and continued to be the chief obstacle to annexation.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> Northern and Southern Democrats followed an unspoken rule: Northerners helped quash anti-slavery proposals and Southerners refrained from agitating for the annexation of Texas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson1984151–152_173-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1984151–152-173">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> Texas withdrew the annexation offer in 1838.<sup id="cite_ref-TexAnnex3638_174-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TexAnnex3638-174">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Border_violence_with_Canada">Border violence with Canada</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Border violence with Canada">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:DENT(1881)_1.213_DESTRUCTION_OF_THE_CAROLINE.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/DENT%281881%29_1.213_DESTRUCTION_OF_THE_CAROLINE.jpg/300px-DENT%281881%29_1.213_DESTRUCTION_OF_THE_CAROLINE.jpg" decoding="async" width="300" height="203" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/DENT%281881%29_1.213_DESTRUCTION_OF_THE_CAROLINE.jpg/450px-DENT%281881%29_1.213_DESTRUCTION_OF_THE_CAROLINE.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/DENT%281881%29_1.213_DESTRUCTION_OF_THE_CAROLINE.jpg/600px-DENT%281881%29_1.213_DESTRUCTION_OF_THE_CAROLINE.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2169" data-file-height="1466" /></a><figcaption>"Destruction of the <i>Caroline</i>", illustration by John Charles Dent (1881)</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Caroline_episode">Caroline episode</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Caroline episode">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Caroline_affair" title="Caroline affair">Caroline affair</a></div>
<p>British subjects in <a href="/wiki/Lower_Canada" title="Lower Canada">Lower Canada</a> (now Quebec) and <a href="/wiki/Upper_Canada" title="Upper Canada">Upper Canada</a> (now Ontario) rose in rebellion in 1837 and 1838, protesting their lack of <a href="/wiki/Responsible_government" title="Responsible government">responsible government</a>. While the initial insurrection in Upper Canada ended quickly (following the December&#160;1837 <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Montgomery%27s_Tavern" title="Battle of Montgomery&#39;s Tavern">Battle of Montgomery's Tavern</a>), many of the rebels fled across the <a href="/wiki/Niagara_River" title="Niagara River">Niagara River</a> into New York, and Upper Canadian rebel leader <a href="/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie" title="William Lyon Mackenzie">William Lyon Mackenzie</a> began recruiting volunteers in <a href="/wiki/Buffalo,_New_York" title="Buffalo, New York">Buffalo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-AoD_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AoD-176">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> Mackenzie declared the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Canada" title="Republic of Canada">Republic of Canada</a> and put into motion a plan whereby volunteers would invade Upper Canada from <a href="/wiki/Navy_Island" title="Navy Island">Navy Island</a> on the Canadian side of the Niagara River. Several hundred volunteers traveled to Navy Island in the weeks that followed. They procured the steamboat <i><a href="/wiki/Caroline_affair" title="Caroline affair">Caroline</a></i> to deliver supplies to Navy Island from <a href="/wiki/Fort_Schlosser" title="Fort Schlosser">Fort Schlosser</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-AoD_176-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AoD-176">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> Seeking to deter an imminent invasion, British forces crossed to the American bank of the river in late December&#160;1837, and they burned and sank the <i>Caroline</i>. In the melee, one American was killed and others were wounded.<sup id="cite_ref-companion_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-companion-177">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Considerable sentiment arose within the United States to declare war, and a British ship was burned in revenge.<sup id="cite_ref-MVforeign_178-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MVforeign-178">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren, looking to avoid a war with Great Britain, sent General Winfield Scott to the <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_border" title="Canada–United States border">Canada–United States border</a> with large discretionary powers for its protection and its peace.<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> Scott impressed upon American citizens the need for a peaceful resolution to the crisis, and made it clear that the U.S. government would not support adventuresome Americans attacking the British. In early January&#160;1838, the president proclaimed neutrality in the Canadian independence issue,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENowlan2012329_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENowlan2012329-180">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> a declaration which Congress endorsed by passing a neutrality law designed to discourage the participation of American citizens in foreign conflicts.<sup id="cite_ref-MVforeign_178-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MVforeign-178">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Patriot_War_of_1837.E2.80.931838"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Patriot_War_of_1837–1838">Patriot War of 1837–1838</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Patriot War of 1837–1838">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Patriot_War" title="Patriot War">Patriot War</a></div>
<p>During the Canadian rebellions, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Duncombe_(Upper_Canada_Rebellion)" title="Charles Duncombe (Upper Canada Rebellion)">Charles Duncombe</a> and <a href="/wiki/Robert_Nelson_(insurrectionist)" title="Robert Nelson (insurrectionist)">Robert Nelson</a> created an armed secret society in Vermont, the <a href="/wiki/Hunters%27_Lodges" title="Hunters&#39; Lodges">Hunters' Lodge</a>. It carried out several small attacks in Upper Canada between December 1837 and December 1838, collectively known as the <a href="/wiki/Patriot_War" title="Patriot War">Patriot War</a>. Washington responded using the Neutrality Act. It prosecuted the leaders and actively deterred Americans from subversive activities abroad. In the long term, Van Buren's opposition to the Patriot War contributed to the construction of healthy <a href="/wiki/Special_Relationship" title="Special Relationship">Anglo-American</a> and <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_relations" title="Canada–United States relations">Canada–United States relations</a>;. It also led, more immediately, to a backlash among citizens regarding the seeming overreach of federal authority,<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> which hurt congressional Democrats in the 1838 midterm elections.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Disputed_Border_in_the_East.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Disputed_Border_in_the_East.jpg/220px-Disputed_Border_in_the_East.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="177" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Disputed_Border_in_the_East.jpg/330px-Disputed_Border_in_the_East.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Disputed_Border_in_the_East.jpg/440px-Disputed_Border_in_the_East.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3813" data-file-height="3072" /></a><figcaption>Rival claims in yellow. The diplomats split the difference along the dotted line.</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span id="Northern_Maine:_the_Aroostook_.22War.22"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Northern_Maine:_the_Aroostook_&quot;War&quot;">Northern Maine: the Aroostook "War"</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Northern Maine: the Aroostook &quot;War&quot;">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>A new crisis surfaced in late 1838, in the disputed territory on the thinly settled <a href="/wiki/History_of_Maine" title="History of Maine">Maine</a>–<a href="/wiki/New_Brunswick" title="New Brunswick">New Brunswick</a> frontier. Americans were settling on long-disputed land claimed by the United States and Great Britain. The British considered possession of the area vital to the defense of Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson1984164–166_182-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1984164–166-182">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> Both American and New Brunswick lumberjack cut timber in the disputed territory during the winter of 1838–1839. On December&#160;29, New Brunswick lumbermen were spotted cutting down trees on an American estate near the <a href="/wiki/Aroostook_River" title="Aroostook River">Aroostook River</a>. When American woodcutters rushed to stand guard, a shouting match, known as the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Caribou" title="Battle of Caribou">Battle of Caribou</a>, ensued. Tensions escalated with officials from both Maine and New Brunswick arresting each other's citizens. <sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> British troops began to gather along the <a href="/wiki/St._John_River_(Bay_of_Fundy)" class="mw-redirect" title="St. John River (Bay of Fundy)">Saint John River</a>. Maine Governor <a href="/wiki/John_Fairfield" title="John Fairfield">John Fairfield</a> mobilized the state militia.  
</p><p>The American press clamored for war; "Maine and her soil, or BLOOD!" screamed one editorial. "Let the sword be drawn and the scabbard thrown away!"<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup>  In June, Congress authorized 50,000&#160;troops and a $10&#160;million budget<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> in the event foreign military troops crossed into United States territory.
</p><p>Van Buren wanted peace and met with the British minister to the United States. The two men agreed to resolve the border issue diplomatically.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESilbey2002128_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESilbey2002128-186">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren sent General Scott to the scene to lower the tensions. Scott successfully convinced all sides to submit the border issue to arbitration. The border dispute was put to rest a few years later, with the signing of the 1842 <a href="/wiki/Webster%E2%80%93Ashburton_Treaty" title="Webster–Ashburton Treaty">Webster–Ashburton Treaty</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-MVforeign_178-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MVforeign-178">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENowlan2012329_180-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENowlan2012329-180">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Amistad_case:_victory_for_the_ex-slaves"><i>Amistad</i> case: victory for the ex-slaves</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Amistad case: victory for the ex-slaves">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/United_States_v._The_Amistad" title="United States v. The Amistad">United States v. The Amistad</a></div>
<p>The Amistad case was a freedom suit that involved international issues and was decided by the U.S. Supreme Court. It resulted from the successful rebellion of African slaves on board the Spanish schooner <i><a href="/wiki/La_Amistad" title="La Amistad">La Amistad</a></i> in 1839. The ship ended up in American waters and was seized by the predecessor agency of the Coast Guard.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren viewed abolitionism as the greatest threat to the nation's unity, and he resisted the slightest interference with slavery in the states where it existed.<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> His administration supported the Spanish government's demand that the ship and its cargo (including the Africans) be turned over to Spain.  However abolitionist lawyers intervened. A federal district court judge ruled that the Africans were legally free and should be transported home, but Van Buren's administration appealed the case to the Supreme Court.<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the Supreme Court in February, 1840, <a href="/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams" title="John Quincy Adams">John Quincy Adams</a> argued passionately for the Africans' right to freedom. Van Buren's Attorney General <a href="/wiki/Henry_D._Gilpin" title="Henry D. Gilpin">Henry D. Gilpin</a> presented the government's case. In March&#160;1841, the Supreme Court issued its final verdict: the <i>Amistad</i> Africans were free people and should be allowed to return home.<sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> The unique nature of the case heightened public interest in the saga, including the participation of former president Adams, Africans testifying in federal court, and their representation by prominent lawyers. Van Buren's administration lost its case and the ex-slaves won. The episode case drew attention to the personal tragedies of slavery and attracted new support for the growing abolition movement in the North. It also transformed the courts into the principal forum for a national debate on the legal foundations of slavery.<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Judicial_appointments">Judicial appointments</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Judicial appointments">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Martin_Van_Buren_judicial_appointments" class="mw-redirect" title="Martin Van Buren judicial appointments">Martin Van Buren judicial appointments</a></div>
<p>Van Buren appointed two Associate Justices to the Supreme Court:<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/John_McKinley" title="John McKinley">John McKinley</a>, confirmed September&#160;25, 1837, and <a href="/wiki/Peter_Vivian_Daniel" class="mw-redirect" title="Peter Vivian Daniel">Peter Vivian Daniel</a>, confirmed March&#160;2, 1841. He also appointed eight other federal judges, all to <a href="/wiki/United_States_district_court" title="United States district court">United States district courts</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="White_House_hostess">White House hostess</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: White House hostess">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>For the first half of his presidency, Van Buren, who had been a widower for many years, did not have a specific person to act as White House hostess at administration social events, but tried to assume such duties himself. When his eldest son <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Van_Buren" title="Abraham Van Buren">Abraham Van Buren</a> married <a href="/wiki/Angelica_Singleton_Van_Buren" title="Angelica Singleton Van Buren">Angelica Singleton</a> in 1838, he quickly acted to install his daughter-in-law as his hostess. She solicited the advice of her distant relative, <a href="/wiki/Dolley_Madison" title="Dolley Madison">Dolley Madison</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-CaroliFirstLadies_194-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CaroliFirstLadies-194">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> who had moved back to Washington after her <a href="/wiki/James_Madison" title="James Madison">husband's</a> death,<sup id="cite_ref-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-195">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> and soon the president's parties livened up. After the 1839 New Year's Eve reception, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Boston_Post" title="The Boston Post">The Boston Post</a></i> raved: "[Angelica Van Buren is a] lady of rare accomplishments, very modest yet perfectly easy and graceful in her manners and free and vivacious in her conversation&#160;... universally admired."<sup id="cite_ref-CaroliFirstLadies_194-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CaroliFirstLadies-194">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As the nation endured a deep <a href="/wiki/Economic_depression" title="Economic depression">economic depression</a>, Angelica Van Buren's receiving style at receptions was influenced by her heavy reading about European court life (and her naive delight in being received as the <i>Queen of the United States</i> when she visited the royal courts of England and France after her marriage). Newspaper coverage of this, and the claim that she intended to re-landscape the <a href="/wiki/South_Lawn_(White_House)" class="mw-redirect" title="South Lawn (White House)">White House grounds</a> to resemble the royal gardens of Europe, was used in a political attack on her father-in-law by a Pennsylvania Whig Congressman <a href="/wiki/Charles_Ogle_(politician)" title="Charles Ogle (politician)">Charles Ogle</a>. He referred obliquely to her as part of the presidential "household" in his famous <a href="/wiki/Gold_Spoon_Oration" title="Gold Spoon Oration">Gold Spoon Oration</a>. The attack was delivered in Congress and the depiction of the president as living a royal lifestyle was a primary factor in his defeat for re-election.<sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidential_election_of_1840">Presidential election of 1840</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Presidential election of 1840">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1840_United_States_presidential_election" title="1840 United States presidential election">1840 United States presidential election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ElectoralCollege1840.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/ElectoralCollege1840.svg/250px-ElectoralCollege1840.svg.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="257" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/ElectoralCollege1840.svg/375px-ElectoralCollege1840.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/ElectoralCollege1840.svg/500px-ElectoralCollege1840.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="577" data-file-height="593" /></a><figcaption>1840 electoral vote results</figcaption></figure>
<p>Van Buren easily won renomination for a second term at the <a href="/wiki/1840_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1840 Democratic National Convention">1840 Democratic National Convention</a>, but he and his party faced a difficult <a href="/wiki/1840_United_States_presidential_election" title="1840 United States presidential election">election in 1840</a>. Van Buren's presidency had been a difficult affair, with the U.S. economy mired in a severe downturn, and other divisive issues, such as slavery, western expansion, and tensions with Great Britain, providing opportunities for Van Buren's political opponents—including some of his fellow Democrats—to criticize his actions.<sup id="cite_ref-MVelections_147-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MVelections-147">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Although Van Buren's renomination was never in doubt, Democratic strategists began to question the wisdom of keeping Johnson on the ticket. Even former president Jackson conceded that Johnson was a liability and insisted on former House Speaker <a href="/wiki/James_K._Polk" title="James K. Polk">James K. Polk</a> of Tennessee as Van Buren's new running mate. Van Buren was reluctant to drop Johnson, who was popular with workers and radicals in the North<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECole1984358_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECole1984358-197">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> and added military experience to the ticket, which might prove important against likely Whig nominee <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison" title="William Henry Harrison">William Henry Harrison</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-RMJ9th_139-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RMJ9th-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> Rather than re-nominating Johnson, the Democratic convention decided to allow state Democratic Party leaders to select the vice-presidential candidates for their states.<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Van Buren hoped that the Whigs would nominate Clay for president, which would allow Van Buren to cast the 1840&#160;campaign as a clash between Van Buren's Independent Treasury system and Clay's support for a national bank. However, rather than nominating longtime party spokesmen like Clay and Daniel Webster, the <a href="/wiki/1839_Whig_National_Convention" title="1839 Whig National Convention">1839 Whig National Convention</a> nominated Harrison, who had served in various governmental positions during his career and had earned fame for his military leadership in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Tippecanoe" title="Battle of Tippecanoe">Battle of Tippecanoe</a> and the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a>. Whig leaders like <a href="/wiki/William_Seward" class="mw-redirect" title="William Seward">William Seward</a> and <a href="/wiki/Thaddeus_Stevens" title="Thaddeus Stevens">Thaddeus Stevens</a> believed that Harrison's war record would effectively counter the popular appeals of the Democratic Party. For vice president, the Whigs nominated former Senator <a href="/wiki/John_Tyler" title="John Tyler">John Tyler</a> of Virginia. Clay was deeply disappointed by his defeat at the convention, but he nonetheless threw his support behind Harrison.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson1984191–195_199-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1984191–195-199">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Whigs presented Harrison as the antithesis of the president, whom they derided as ineffective, corrupt, and effete.<sup id="cite_ref-MVelections_147-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MVelections-147">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Whigs also depicted Van Buren as an aristocrat living in high style in the White House, while they used images of Harrison in a log cabin sipping cider to convince voters that he was a man of the people.<sup id="cite_ref-NPSsetting_200-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NPSsetting-200">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> They threw such jabs as "Van, Van, is a used-up man" and "Martin Van Ruin" and ridiculed him in newspapers and cartoons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEManweller2012&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksiduqB3ehA7M0oCpgPA278_278&#93;_201-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEManweller2012[httpsbooksgooglecombooksiduqB3ehA7M0oCpgPA278_278]-201">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> Issues of policy were not absent from the campaign; the Whigs derided the alleged executive overreaches of Jackson and Van Buren, while also calling for a national bank and higher tariffs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson1984199–200_202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1984199–200-202">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> Democrats attempted to campaign on the Independent Treasury system, but the onset of <a href="/wiki/Deflation" title="Deflation">deflation</a> undercut these arguments.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson1984203–204_203-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1984203–204-203">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> The enthusiasm for "<a href="/wiki/Tippecanoe_and_Tyler_Too" title="Tippecanoe and Tyler Too">Tippecanoe and Tyler Too</a>", coupled with the country's severe economic crisis, made it impossible for Van Buren to win a second term.<sup id="cite_ref-NPSsetting_200-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NPSsetting-200">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> Harrison won by a popular vote of 1,275,612 to 1,130,033, and an electoral vote margin of 234 to 60.<sup id="cite_ref-preselections_141-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-preselections-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> An astonishing 80% of eligible voters went to the polls on election day.<sup id="cite_ref-MVelections_147-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MVelections-147">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren actually won more votes than he had in 1836, but the Whig success in attracting new voters more than canceled out Democratic gains.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson1984206–207_204-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1984206–207-204">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> Additionally, Whigs won majorities for the first time in both the House of Representatives and the Senate.<sup id="cite_ref-RMJ9th_139-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RMJ9th-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Post-presidency_.281841.E2.80.931862.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-presidency_(1841–1862)">Post-presidency (1841–1862)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Post-presidency (1841–1862)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_of_1844">Election of 1844</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Election of 1844">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>On the expiration of his term, Van Buren returned to his estate of <a href="/wiki/Lindenwald" class="mw-redirect" title="Lindenwald">Lindenwald</a> in Kinderhook.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983478–479_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983478–479-205">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> He continued to closely watch political developments, including the battle between the Whig alliance of the Great Triumvirate and President <a href="/wiki/John_Tyler" title="John Tyler">John Tyler</a>, who took office after Harrison's death in April&#160;1841.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983487–488_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983487–488-206">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> Though undecided on another presidential run, Van Buren made several moves calculated to maintain his support, including a trip to the <a href="/wiki/Southern_United_States" title="Southern United States">Southern United States</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Western_United_States" title="Western United States">Western United States</a> during which he met with Jackson, former Speaker of the House <a href="/wiki/James_K._Polk" title="James K. Polk">James K. Polk</a>, and others.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983488–494_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983488–494-207">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> President Tyler, <a href="/wiki/James_Buchanan" title="James Buchanan">James Buchanan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Levi_Woodbury" title="Levi Woodbury">Levi Woodbury</a>, and others loomed as potential challengers for the 1844&#160;Democratic nomination, but it was Calhoun who posed the most formidable obstacle.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983501_208-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983501-208">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Van Buren remained silent on major public issues like the debate over the <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_1842" title="Tariff of 1842">Tariff of 1842</a>, hoping to arrange for the appearance of a draft movement for his presidential candidacy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983504–505_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983504–505-209">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> Tyler made the annexation of Texas his chief foreign policy goal, and many Democrats, particularly in the South, were anxious to quickly complete it.<sup id="cite_ref-rlambert1_210-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rlambert1-210">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> After an explosion on the <a href="/wiki/USS_Princeton_(1843)" title="USS Princeton (1843)">USS&#160;<i>Princeton</i></a> killed Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/Abel_P._Upshur" title="Abel P. Upshur">Abel P. Upshur</a> in February&#160;1844, Tyler brought Calhoun into his cabinet to direct foreign affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983516–520_211-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983516–520-211">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> Like Tyler, Calhoun pursued the annexation of Texas to upend the presidential race and to extend slavery into new territories.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983518–520_212-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983518–520-212">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Shortly after taking office, Calhoun negotiated an annexation treaty between the United States and Texas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983520_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983520-213">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren had hoped he would not have to take a public stand on annexation, but as the Texas question came to dominate U.S. politics, he decided to make his views on the issue public.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983520–521,_525_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983520–521,_525-214">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> Though he believed that his public acceptance of annexation would likely help him win the 1844 Democratic nomination, Van Buren thought that annexation would inevitably lead to an unjust war with Mexico.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983525–527_215-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983525–527-215">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> In a public letter published shortly after Henry Clay also announced his opposition to the annexation treaty, Van Buren articulated his views on the Texas question.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983528–529_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983528–529-216">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Van Buren's opposition to immediate annexation cost him the support of many pro-slavery Democrats.<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> In the weeks before the <a href="/wiki/1844_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1844 Democratic National Convention">1844 Democratic National Convention</a>, Van Buren's supporters anticipated that he would win a majority of the delegates on the first presidential ballot, but would not be able to win the support of the required two-thirds of delegates.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983533–534_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983533–534-218">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren's supporters attempted to prevent the adoption of the two-thirds rule, but several Northern delegates joined with Southern delegates in implementing the two-thirds rule for the 1844 convention.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983535–538_219-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983535–538-219">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren won 146 of the 266&#160;votes on the first presidential ballot, with only 12 of his votes coming from Southern states.<sup id="cite_ref-rlambert1_210-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rlambert1-210">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Senator <a href="/wiki/Lewis_Cass" title="Lewis Cass">Lewis Cass</a> won much of the remaining vote, and he gradually picked up support on subsequent ballots until the convention adjourned for the day.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983538_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983538-220">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> When the convention reconvened and held another ballot, James K. Polk, who shared many of Van Buren's views but favored immediate annexation, won 44&#160;votes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983539_221-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983539-221">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> On the ninth ballot, Van Buren's supporters withdrew his name from consideration, and Polk won the nomination.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983539–540_222-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983539–540-222">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup> Although angered that his opponents had denied him the nomination, Van Buren endorsed Polk in the interest of party unity.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983541–542_223-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983541–542-223">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup> He also convinced <a href="/wiki/Silas_Wright" title="Silas Wright">Silas Wright</a> to run for Governor of New York so that the popular Wright could help boost Polk in the state.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983547–548_224-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983547–548-224">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> Wright narrowly defeated Whig nominee <a href="/wiki/Millard_Fillmore" title="Millard Fillmore">Millard Fillmore</a> in the <a href="/wiki/New_York_state_election,_1844" class="mw-redirect" title="New York state election, 1844">1844 gubernatorial election</a>, and Wright's victory in the state helped Polk narrowly defeat Henry Clay in the <a href="/wiki/1844_United_States_presidential_election" title="1844 United States presidential election">1844 presidential election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983548_225-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983548-225">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After taking office, Polk used <a href="/wiki/George_Bancroft" title="George Bancroft">George Bancroft</a> as an intermediary to offer Van Buren the ambassadorship to London. Van Buren declined, partly because he was upset with Polk over the treatment the Van Buren delegates had received at the 1844 convention, and partly because he was content in his retirement.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECole1984405_226-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECole1984405-226">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> Polk also consulted Van Buren in the formation of his cabinet, but offended Van Buren by offering to appoint a New Yorker only to the lesser post of Secretary of War, rather than as Secretary of State or Secretary of the Treasury.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983550–556_227-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983550–556-227">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> Other patronage decisions also angered Van Buren and Wright, and they became permanently alienated from the Polk administration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983564–565_228-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983564–565-228">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_of_1848">Election of 1848</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Election of 1848">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Martin_Van_Buren_daguerreotype_by_Mathew_Brady_circa_1849_-_edit_1.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Half-length photographic portrait of an elderly, balding man dressed in a dark coat, vest and cravat" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/Martin_Van_Buren_daguerreotype_by_Mathew_Brady_circa_1849_-_edit_1.jpg/180px-Martin_Van_Buren_daguerreotype_by_Mathew_Brady_circa_1849_-_edit_1.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="247" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/Martin_Van_Buren_daguerreotype_by_Mathew_Brady_circa_1849_-_edit_1.jpg/270px-Martin_Van_Buren_daguerreotype_by_Mathew_Brady_circa_1849_-_edit_1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/Martin_Van_Buren_daguerreotype_by_Mathew_Brady_circa_1849_-_edit_1.jpg/360px-Martin_Van_Buren_daguerreotype_by_Mathew_Brady_circa_1849_-_edit_1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1120" data-file-height="1536" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Daguerreotype" title="Daguerreotype">Daguerreotype</a> of Van Buren by <a href="/wiki/Mathew_Brady" title="Mathew Brady">Mathew Brady</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1849–1850</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>Though he had previously helped maintain a balance between the <a href="/wiki/Barnburners_and_Hunkers" title="Barnburners and Hunkers">Barnburners and Hunkers</a>, the two factions of the New York Democratic Party, Van Buren moved closer to the Barnburners after the 1844 Democratic National Convention.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983543–544_229-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983543–544-229">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> The split in the state party worsened during Polk's presidency, as his administration lavished patronage on the Hunkers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983570–571_230-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983570–571-230">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> In his retirement, Van Buren also grew increasingly opposed to slavery.<sup id="cite_ref-231" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-231">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As the <a href="/wiki/Mexican%E2%80%93American_War" title="Mexican–American War">Mexican–American War</a> brought the debate over slavery in the territories to the forefront of American politics, Van Buren published an anti-slavery manifesto. In it, he refuted the notion that Congress did not have the power to regulate slavery in the territories, and argued the <a href="/wiki/Founding_Fathers" class="mw-redirect" title="Founding Fathers">Founding Fathers</a> had favored the eventual <a href="/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States" title="Abolitionism in the United States">abolition</a> of slavery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983567–570_232-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983567–570-232">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup> The document, which became known as the "Barnburner Manifesto," was edited at Van Buren's request by John Van Buren and <a href="/wiki/Samuel_Tilden" class="mw-redirect" title="Samuel Tilden">Samuel Tilden</a>, both of whom were leaders of the Barnburner faction.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESilbey2002193–194_233-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESilbey2002193–194-233">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> After the publication of the Barnburner Manifesto, many Barnburners urged the former president to seek his old office in the <a href="/wiki/1848_United_States_presidential_election" title="1848 United States presidential election">1848 presidential election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983575–576_234-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983575–576-234">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/1848_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1848 Democratic National Convention">1848 Democratic National Convention</a> seated competing Barnburner and Hunker delegations from New York, but the Barnburners walked out of the convention when Lewis Cass, who opposed congressional regulation of slavery in the territories, was nominated on the fourth ballot.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983580_235-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983580-235">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In response to the nomination of Cass, the Barnburners began to organize as a <a href="/wiki/Third_party_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Third party (United States)">third party</a>. At a convention held in June&#160;1848, in <a href="/wiki/Utica,_New_York" title="Utica, New York">Utica, New York</a>, the Barnburners nominated Van Buren for president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983581–585_236-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983581–585-236">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup> Though reluctant to bolt from the Democratic Party, Van Buren accepted the nomination to show the power of the anti-slavery movement, help defeat Cass, and weaken the Hunkers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983585–586_237-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983585–586-237">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> At a convention held in <a href="/wiki/Buffalo,_New_York" title="Buffalo, New York">Buffalo, New York</a> in August&#160;1848, a group of anti-slavery Democrats, Whigs, and members of the abolitionist <a href="/wiki/Liberty_Party_(United_States,_1840)" title="Liberty Party (United States, 1840)">Liberty Party</a> met in the first national convention of what became known as the <a href="/wiki/Free_Soil_Party" title="Free Soil Party">Free Soil Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983587–589_238-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983587–589-238">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The convention unanimously nominated Van Buren, and chose <a href="/wiki/Charles_Francis_Adams_Sr." title="Charles Francis Adams Sr.">Charles Francis Adams</a> as Van Buren's running mate. In a public message accepting the nomination, Van Buren gave his full support for the <a href="/wiki/Wilmot_Proviso" title="Wilmot Proviso">Wilmot Proviso</a>, a proposed law that would ban slavery in all territories acquired from Mexico in the Mexican–American War.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983587–589_238-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983587–589-238">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup> Anti-slavery Whig orator <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Webster" title="Daniel Webster">Daniel Webster</a>, in his "Marshfield Speech," expressed skepticism, in terms that may have influenced Whig voters, about the sincerity of Van Buren's espousal of the anti-slavery cause:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>In Mr. Van Buren's anti-slavery professions, Mr. Webster had no confidence. He said pleasantly, but significantly, that "if he and Mr. Van Buren should meet under the Free-soil flag, the latter with his accustomed good-nature would laugh." He added, with a touch of characteristic humor, "that the leader of the Free-spoil party suddenly becoming the leader of the Free-soil party is a joke to shake his sides and mine."<sup id="cite_ref-Blaine_239-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Blaine-239">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Van Buren won no electoral votes, but finished second to Whig nominee <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor" title="Zachary Taylor">Zachary Taylor</a> in New York, taking enough votes from Cass to give the state—and perhaps the election—to Taylor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEManweller2012&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksidyM7E2G0tAiwCpgPA271_271&#93;_240-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEManweller2012[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidyM7E2G0tAiwCpgPA271_271]-240">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup> Nationwide, Van Buren won 10.1% of the popular vote, the <a href="/wiki/List_of_third_party_performances_in_United_States_presidential_elections" title="List of third party performances in United States presidential elections">strongest showing by a third-party presidential nominee</a> up to that point in U.S. history.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Retirement">Retirement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Retirement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Martin_Van_Buren_daguerreotype-restored.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Half-length photographic portrait of an elderly, balding man dressed in a dark coat, vest and cravat" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Martin_Van_Buren_daguerreotype-restored.jpg/180px-Martin_Van_Buren_daguerreotype-restored.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="219" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Martin_Van_Buren_daguerreotype-restored.jpg/270px-Martin_Van_Buren_daguerreotype-restored.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/de/Martin_Van_Buren_daguerreotype-restored.jpg 2x" data-file-width="357" data-file-height="434" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Daguerreotype" title="Daguerreotype">Daguerreotype</a> of Martin Van Buren, circa 1855</figcaption></figure>
<p>Van Buren never sought public office again after the 1848 election, but he continued to closely follow national politics. He was deeply troubled by the stirrings of secessionism in the South and welcomed the <a href="/wiki/Compromise_of_1850" title="Compromise of 1850">Compromise of 1850</a> as a necessary conciliatory measure despite his opposition to the <a href="/wiki/Fugitive_Slave_Act_of_1850" title="Fugitive Slave Act of 1850">Fugitive Slave Act of 1850</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983592_241-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983592-241">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren also worked on a history of American political parties and embarked on a tour of Europe, becoming the first former U.S. President to visit Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983598–602_242-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983598–602-242">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup> Though still concerned about slavery, Van Buren and his followers returned to the Democratic fold, partly out of the fear that a continuing Democratic split would help the Whig Party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESilbey2002203_243-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESilbey2002203-243">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup> He also attempted to reconcile the Barnburners and the Hunkers, with mixed results.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESilbey2002204_244-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESilbey2002204-244">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Van Buren supported <a href="/wiki/Franklin_Pierce" title="Franklin Pierce">Franklin Pierce</a> for president in <a href="/wiki/1852_United_States_presidential_election" title="1852 United States presidential election">1852</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983596_245-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983596-245">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup> James Buchanan in <a href="/wiki/1856_United_States_presidential_election" title="1856 United States presidential election">1856</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983605_246-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983605-246">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Stephen_A._Douglas" title="Stephen A. Douglas">Stephen A. Douglas</a> in <a href="/wiki/1860_United_States_presidential_election" title="1860 United States presidential election">1860</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983610_247-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983610-247">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren viewed the fledgling <a href="/wiki/Know_Nothing" title="Know Nothing">Know Nothing</a> movement with contempt and felt that the anti-slavery <a href="/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States)" title="Republican Party (United States)">Republican Party</a> exacerbated sectional tensions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983604–605_248-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983604–605-248">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup> He considered Chief Justice <a href="/wiki/Roger_Taney" class="mw-redirect" title="Roger Taney">Roger Taney</a>'s ruling in the 1857 case of <i><a href="/wiki/Dred_Scott_v._Sandford" title="Dred Scott v. Sandford">Dred Scott v. Sandford</a></i> to be a "grievous mistake" since it overturned the <a href="/wiki/Missouri_Compromise" title="Missouri Compromise">Missouri Compromise</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983605–606_249-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983605–606-249">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup> He assessed that the Buchanan administration handled the issue of <a href="/wiki/Bleeding_Kansas" title="Bleeding Kansas">Bleeding Kansas</a> poorly, and saw the <a href="/wiki/Lecompton_Constitution" title="Lecompton Constitution">Lecompton Constitution</a> as a sop to Southern extremists.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983607_250-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983607-250">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mvanburen.jpeg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A three-quarters length painted portrait of a balding man with gray hair, standing with his right hand grasping a bundle of papers lying on a table" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Mvanburen.jpeg/180px-Mvanburen.jpeg" decoding="async" width="180" height="241" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Mvanburen.jpeg/270px-Mvanburen.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Mvanburen.jpeg/360px-Mvanburen.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="2242" data-file-height="3000" /></a><figcaption>1858 portrait by <a href="/wiki/George_Peter_Alexander_Healy" title="George Peter Alexander Healy">GPA Healy</a>, on display at the <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>After the election of <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln" title="Abraham Lincoln">Abraham Lincoln</a> and the secession of several Southern states in 1860, Van Buren unsuccessfully sought to call a constitutional convention.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1983610_247-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1983610-247">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup> In April&#160;1861, former president Pierce wrote to the other living former presidents and asked them to consider meeting to use their stature and influence to propose a negotiated end to the war. Pierce asked Van Buren to use his role as the senior living ex-president to issue a formal call. Van Buren's reply suggested that Buchanan should be the one to call the meeting, since he was the former president who had served most recently, or that Pierce should issue the call himself if he strongly believed in the merit of his proposal. Neither Buchanan nor Pierce was willing to make Pierce's proposal public, and nothing more resulted from it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECole1984425_251-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECole1984425-251">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup> Once the <a href="/wiki/American_Civil_War" title="American Civil War">American Civil War</a> began, Van Buren made public his support for the <a href="/wiki/Union_(American_Civil_War)" title="Union (American Civil War)">Union</a> cause.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWidmer2005164_252-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWidmer2005164-252">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><span class="anchor" id="Death"></span>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Martin_van_Buren%27s_grave_at_the_Reformed_Dutch_Cemetery_in_Kinderhook._-_panoramio.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Martin_van_Buren%27s_grave_at_the_Reformed_Dutch_Cemetery_in_Kinderhook._-_panoramio.jpg/180px-Martin_van_Buren%27s_grave_at_the_Reformed_Dutch_Cemetery_in_Kinderhook._-_panoramio.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="240" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Martin_van_Buren%27s_grave_at_the_Reformed_Dutch_Cemetery_in_Kinderhook._-_panoramio.jpg/270px-Martin_van_Buren%27s_grave_at_the_Reformed_Dutch_Cemetery_in_Kinderhook._-_panoramio.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Martin_van_Buren%27s_grave_at_the_Reformed_Dutch_Cemetery_in_Kinderhook._-_panoramio.jpg/360px-Martin_van_Buren%27s_grave_at_the_Reformed_Dutch_Cemetery_in_Kinderhook._-_panoramio.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1536" data-file-height="2048" /></a><figcaption>Van Buren's grave in Kinderhook</figcaption></figure>
<p>Van Buren's health began to fail later in 1861, and he was bedridden with <a href="/wiki/Pneumonia" title="Pneumonia">pneumonia</a> during the fall and winter of 1861–1862.<sup id="cite_ref-253" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-253">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup> He died of bronchial <a href="/wiki/Asthma" title="Asthma">asthma</a> and heart failure at his Lindenwald estate at 2:00&#160;a.m. on Thursday, July&#160;24, 1862. He was 79 years old.<sup id="cite_ref-254" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-254">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup> He is buried in the <a href="/wiki/Reformed_Dutch_Church_(Kinderhook,_New_York)" title="Reformed Dutch Church (Kinderhook, New York)">Kinderhook Reformed Dutch Church</a> Cemetery, as are his wife Hannah, his parents, and his son Martin Van Buren Jr.<sup id="cite_ref-255" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-255">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Van Buren outlived all four of his immediate successors: Harrison, Tyler, Polk, and Taylor.<sup id="cite_ref-256" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-256">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup> In addition, he saw more successors ascend to the presidency than anyone else (eight), living to see Abraham Lincoln elected as the 16th President before his death.<sup id="cite_ref-257" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-257">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Historical_reputation">Historical reputation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Martin_Van_Buren&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Historical reputation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Van Buren's most lasting achievement was as a political organizer who built the Democratic Party and guided it to dominance in the <a href="/wiki/Second_Party_System" title="Second Party System">Second Party System</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECole198416_258-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECole198416-258">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup> and historians have come to regard Van Buren as integral to the development of the American political system.<sup id="cite_ref-MVLiB_31-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MVLiB-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> According to historian <a href="/wiki/Robert_V._Remini" title="Robert V. Remini">Robert V. Remini</a>:
</p>
<blockquote><p>Van Buren's creative contribution to the political development of the nation was enormous, and as such he earned his way to the presidency. After gaining control of New York's Republican Party he organized the Albany Regency to run the state in his absence while he pursued a national career in Washington. The Regency was a governing consul in Albany consisting of a group of politically astute and highly intelligent men. He was one of the first statewide political machines in the country was success resulted from its professional use of patronage, the legislative caucus, and the official party newspaper.....[In Washington] he labored to bring about the reorganization of the Republican Party through an alliance between what he called "the planters of the South and the plain Republicans of the North."... [His Democratic] emphasized the importance of building popular majorities and it perfected political techniques which would appeal to the masses....Heretofore parties were regarded as evils to be tolerated; Van Buren argued that the party system was the most sensible and intelligent way the affairs of the nation could be democratically conducted, a viewpoint that eventually won national approval.<sup id="cite_ref-259" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-259">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:MVanBuren.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/MVanBuren.png/180px-MVanBuren.png" decoding="async" width="180" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/MVanBuren.png/270px-MVanBuren.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/MVanBuren.png/360px-MVanBuren.png 2x" data-file-width="2405" data-file-height="3029" /></a><figcaption>Gubernatorial <a href="/wiki/Portrait" title="Portrait">portrait</a> of Martin Van Buren by <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Huntington_(artist)" title="Daniel Huntington (artist)">Daniel Huntington</a> in The Civil War</figcaption></figure>
<p>However, his presidency is <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">considered to be average</a>, at best, by historians. He was blamed for the Panic of 1837 and defeated for reelection.<sup id="cite_ref-MVLiB_31-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MVLiB-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> His tenure was dominated by the economic conditions caused by the panic, and historians have split on the adequacy of the Independent Treasury as a response.<sup id="cite_ref-260" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-260">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup> Some historians disagree with these negative assessments. For example, libertarian writer <a href="/wiki/Ivan_Eland" title="Ivan Eland">Ivan Eland</a>, in his 2009 book <i><a href="/wiki/Recarving_Rushmore" title="Recarving Rushmore">Recarving Rushmore</a></i>, ranked Van Buren as the third greatest president.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_261-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-261">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:1_262-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-262">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup> He argues that Van Buren handled the Panic of 1837 well, and praised Van Buren for reducing <a href="/wiki/Government_spending" title="Government spending">government spending</a>, <a href="/wiki/Balanced_budget" title="Balanced budget">balancing the budget</a>, and avoiding potential wars with Canada and Mexico.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_261-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-261">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:1_262-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-262">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup> Historian Jeffrey Rogers Hummel states that Van Buren was America's greatest president, arguing that "historians have grossly underrated his many remarkable accomplishments in the face of heavy odds".<sup id="cite_ref-263" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-263">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Several writers have portrayed Van Buren as among the nation's most obscure presidents. As noted in a 2014 <i>Time</i> magazine article on the "Top 10 Forgettable Presidents":
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Making himself nearly disappear completely from the history books was probably not the trick the "Little Magician" Martin Van Buren had in mind, but his was the first truly forgettable American presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-264" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-264">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
